		<item>
		<title>Introducing managed cluster extensions for observability and user management in Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Slurm</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-managed-cluster-extensions-for-observability-and-user-management-in-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-slurm/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Austin Cherian]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 20 Nov 2026 23:07:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d2380cae67e4f5d5655c6b63e914ed4170fdafd6</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker HyperPod now offers managed cluster extensions for observability and user management, bringing declarative, API- and console-driven configuration to the software running on Slurm cluster nodes. This post explains how the extensions work and follows a platform team through a quarter of running a shared Slurm cluster with them.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Keeping a shared Slurm cluster for foundation model development in sync entails keeping several separately maintained pieces of configuration matched to what’s running on the nodes. The cluster’s shape lives on multiple configuration files, its software in custom scripts, its network topology in files that must be edited as nodes change, and its users in a roster that an administrator must maintain and rerun. Over time these pieces drift away from what’s actually running, and nothing tells you when they do.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We believe cluster configurations should be managed the way you manage the rest of your AWS infrastructure. You declare what you want through an API or the console, AWS validates it before it’s applied, the cluster is kept in that state continuously, and you can see whether it’s working. During 2026, Amazon SageMaker HyperPod brought that approach to Slurm clusters, with API-driven Slurm configuration, AMI-based configuration, continuous provisioning, automatic topology management, and deep health checks. Today, we launched cluster extensions, which bring the same approach to the software and settings on running nodes, with managed extensions for observability and user management. However, through all of it, you still keep full access to every node in your cluster, including the controller node. You can SSH in, configure nodes directly, and run your own lifecycle scripts. The APIs give you less to maintain, direct access gives you more control, and you choose the balance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the features we introduced in 2026 and explain why the software on running nodes needed a different mechanism. We then show how cluster extensions work, through both the API and the console, and follow a platform team through a quarter on a cluster that uses them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="a-declarative-foundation-for-slurm-cluster-operations"&gt;A declarative foundation for Slurm cluster operations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We can think of a Slurm cluster’s configuration grouped into six layers. Five of them describe the cluster itself: its definition, node setup, capacity, network topology, and hardware health. The sixth covers the software running on its nodes, which we discuss in the next section.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 410px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/image_43512305-1.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/image_43512305-1.jpg" alt="Flow from HyperPod recording an extension’s desired configuration to a node agent applying the extension package on every node, including new and replaced nodes, with health probes reporting status and health issues back" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The Slurm cluster configuration stack&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Earlier in 2026, we brought declarative management to the first five layers. With &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/02/amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-slurm/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;API-driven Slurm configuration&lt;/a&gt;, administrators define node roles, partitions, and Amazon FSx for Lustre and Amazon FSx for OpenZFS mounts in CreateCluster and UpdateCluster requests, and connect &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-external-accounting-db-slurm.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;an external accounting database&lt;/a&gt;. HyperPod updates partition settings while preserving other controller-node Slurm customizations. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/05/amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-ami-based-node/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AMI-based configuration&lt;/a&gt; replaces routine setup scripts with images preconfigured with Slurm, Docker, Enroot, Pyxis, and home directories. A &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/07/hyperpod-custom-ami-slurm/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;custom AMI&lt;/a&gt; or OnInitComplete script handles organization-specific software. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/03/amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-continuous-provisioning/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Continuous provisioning for Slurm&lt;/a&gt; retries capacity requests in the background; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/06/ami-configuration-continuous-slurm/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AMI-based configuration works with it&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/05/amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-mincount/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;minimum capacity requirements&lt;/a&gt; let teams specify MinInstanceCount, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/11/sagemaker-hyperpod-cluster-ops/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cluster operations&lt;/a&gt; track scaling requests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/04/amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-automatic-slurm-topology/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automatic topology management&lt;/a&gt; maintains the network layout as nodes scale or are replaced. On Slurm 25.11 or later, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/07/hyperpod-partition-topology-slurm/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;partition-level topology&lt;/a&gt; supports tree or block topology per partition. For hardware health, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/04/amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-on-demand-deep-health-check/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;on-demand deep health checks&lt;/a&gt; stress GPUs and test NVIDIA Collective Communications Library (NCCL) connectivity, while &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/07/deep-health-check-continuous-slurm/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deep health checks with continuous provisioning&lt;/a&gt; run as nodes are provisioned. Nodes under check stay in Slurm maintenance reservations until checks finish, keeping jobs off them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="introducing-cluster-extensions"&gt;Introducing cluster extensions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The five layers described so far are configurations that HyperPod can derive or apply once, when it provisions a node. The sixth layer, the software that runs on the nodes, behaves differently because it keeps changing for the life of the cluster. As researchers join and leave, a team asks for deeper GPU metrics for a week, and an exporter ships a fix. For this layer, lifecycle scripts have been the main mechanism so far, and they have drawbacks when the software keeps changing. A script runs only when a node is provisioned, so each change means editing it and then replacing nodes or rerunning it across the fleet. A mistake shows only when the script runs on a node, and nothing reports what’s installed, so nodes drift apart over time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We wanted the software to be declared in one place, checked when you submit it, kept consistent across nodes, and visible afterward. In November 2026, we launched cluster extensions, which are software packages managed by AWS that you install, configure, update, and remove through APIs or the Amazon SageMaker AI console, starting with observability and user management, including directory integration, as the first software extensions. Compared with scripts, extensions have these advantages:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validated up front:&lt;/strong&gt; HyperPod checks an extension’s settings against its schema when you submit them, through the API or the console, so a misspelled setting is rejected before anything reaches a node.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Consistent on every node:&lt;/strong&gt; HyperPod applies the extension to every node, including nodes added by scale-up or replacement, and keeps the nodes consistent as the cluster scales and heals.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Changed through the API or the console:&lt;/strong&gt; You change a setting or upgrade a version with an API call, or from the SageMaker AI console, instead of editing scripts and replacing nodes. Both paths use the same validated configuration, and this post shows console screenshots alongside the API examples.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Visible:&lt;/strong&gt; Each extension reports its status and per-node health.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use cluster extensions when HyperPod offers a managed software capability. Use custom AMIs and lifecycle scripts for organization-specific software or initialization that the available extensions don’t cover. HyperPod supports two cluster managed extensions today:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The observability extension (sagemaker-observability) collects node, GPU, Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA), Slurm job, and Slurm cluster metrics and sends them to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/prometheus/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user management extension (sagemaker-user-management) creates users and groups with the same IDs on every node, controls who can use SSH and sudo on each node type, and registers users with Slurm accounting. It can also connect the cluster to Active Directory, for example &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/directoryservice/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Directory Service for Microsoft Active Directory&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows how an extension reaches your nodes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-2.png" alt="Flow from HyperPod recording an extension’s desired configuration to a node agent applying the extension package on every node, including new and replaced nodes, with health probes reporting status and health issues back" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: HyperPod can install, edit, or remove an extension. HyperPod records the desired configuration, a node agent applies the extension package on every node, including new and replaced ones, and health probes report status and health issues back&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="using-cluster-extensions"&gt;Using cluster extensions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can use cluster extensions from the SageMaker AI console or through an API. On the console, the Extensions tab on a cluster’s details page lists each extension with its status, version, and ARN, and it’s where you install a new one. Each extension adds controls of its own, such as the metric store and an open dashboard in Grafana button for observability, and the number of users and groups and a Manage users button for user management. You can also manage every extension through APIs, from the AWS CLI or an SDK, to script installs, keep settings in version control, repeat a setup across clusters, or check status and health from your own tooling. The following table lists the six cluster extension APIs, from installing an extension to listing the versions you can upgrade to, along with console view for extensions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_145946" style="width: 1044px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-145946" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-145946" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ml-22428-figure-3.png" alt="" width="1034" height="240"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-145946" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Left, the six cluster extension APIs and what each one does. Right, the console view to add and remove extensions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows what happens after you call one of these APIs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-4.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-4.jpg" alt="Flow showing an extension’s version and settings declared through the API, validated and recorded by HyperPod, applied to every node for its role, with status and health reported back" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: You declare an extension’s version and settings through the API. HyperPod validates and records them, applies them to every node for its role, and reports status and health&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you create or update an extension, HyperPod validates your settings against the version’s schema during the API call, so a misspelled setting is rejected immediately. It then records the version and settings as the desired state and applies them, with each node installing only what its role needs, such as GPU exporters on GPU nodes only. New and replacement nodes get the same state a few minutes after they start, and every node reports whether the extension applied and whether it’s healthy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Updates send only the settings that you want to change, and HyperPod merges them into the stored settings. Problems that show up only on a node, such as a port or user ID that something else already uses, appear in the Health field of DescribeClusterExtension, grouped by cause with a code, a message, and a count of affected nodes. HyperPod retries internal failures on its own, and for anything else you fix the cause and call UpdateClusterExtension with no changes to retry the affected nodes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An extension’s status is Creating or Updating while a change rolls out, Active when every node runs the requested version and settings and none reports a problem, and Degraded when some nodes failed or report a problem. Each extension has its own ARN, so a platform team can own sagemaker-user-management while a monitoring team owns sagemaker-observability. Each health issue names the component, describes the effect on your cluster, gives the next step, and counts the affected nodes, so you can see where to look without signing in to each node, as the following figure shows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-5.png" alt="An extension’s status display with an example health issue for a node exporter that isn’t running" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: An extension’s status, and an example health issue for a node exporter that isn’t running&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="when-to-use-cluster-extensions-and-when-to-use-lifecycle-scripts"&gt;When to use cluster extensions and when to use lifecycle scripts&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A useful way to decide is to ask who owns the software and how often it changes. Some of the software on a cluster is specific to your organization, such as security agents, license servers, and the storage layout your data pipeline expects. Lifecycle scripts and custom AMIs are the right tools for it, because only your team knows what it needs. The rest is software that almost every cluster needs, like GPU and job metrics and user accounts with consistent IDs and access rules. That software must match on every node and keeps changing, so cluster extensions are the better fit, because you declare it once, HyperPod validates it, and each extension reports whether it’s healthy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two approaches can coexist, but each component should have one owner. Don’t manage the same exporter, user, or service through both a lifecycle script and an extension, because competing configurations can cause installation or runtime conflicts. Cluster extensions work with every lifecycle configuration option.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you already run these capabilities from scripts, you can move to extensions one capability at a time, and scripts and extensions can run side by side while you do. For each capability, the migration takes four steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Call UpdateClusterSoftware to move the cluster to the November 2026 HyperPod AMI or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn the capability off in your lifecycle scripts and upload them to your S3 bucket, so that new nodes skip it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;During a planned maintenance window, stop and remove the script-installed agents on every node. This is the only manual, fleet-wide step.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Call CreateClusterExtension to install the extension on the clean cluster.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If anything is left behind, the install names the conflict, such as a port that’s already in use, and DescribeClusterExtension shows it under Health. With user management, you keep the same usernames and user IDs. The following figure shows four steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-6.png" alt="The four steps to move an existing cluster from lifecycle scripts to extensions, one capability at a time" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Moving an existing cluster from lifecycle scripts to extensions, one capability at a time&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-you-need-to-get-started-with-cluster-extensions"&gt;What you need to get started with cluster extensions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You need a HyperPod Slurm cluster on or after the November 2, 2026 HyperPod AMI or later with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-scaling-slurm.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;continuous provisioning enabled&lt;/a&gt;. An extension runs software as root on every node, so treat CreateClusterExtension, UpdateClusterExtension, and DeleteClusterExtension as administrator permissions. We suggest that you do not grant them to the cluster’s execution role, because every user who can sign in to the nodes can use its credentials. That role does need access to the destinations that the extensions use, which means Amazon CloudWatch or aps:RemoteWrite for observability and secretsmanager:GetSecretValue for the Active Directory secrets that user management reads. If those secrets are encrypted with a customer managed AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) key, the role also needs kms:Decrypt on that key. User management also needs a shared file system, such as Amazon FSx for Lustre, for home directories.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For procedures, IAM policies, and configuration references, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-slurm-extensions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cluster extensions for Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Slurm clusters&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="a-platform-teams-journey-managing-a-shared-slurm-cluster-using-cluster-extensions"&gt;A platform team’s journey managing a shared Slurm cluster using cluster extensions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To see how cluster extensions are used in practice, consider a platform team that manages a shared Slurm cluster for several research groups. The team needs a reviewable cluster definition, GPU and job metrics, corporate directory access, local accounts for a partner lab, and separate ownership of observability and user management. The following examples show how the team configures these capabilities and responds to routine changes and failures.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="create-the-cluster-from-one-definition"&gt;Create the cluster from one definition&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The administrator declares the whole cluster, including node roles, partitions, FSx mounts, minimum capacity, deep health checks, and the external accounting database, in a single CreateCluster request. For a complete example request, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/smcluster-getting-started-slurm-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with SageMaker HyperPod using the AWS CLI&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The request has no lifecycle configuration, topology file, or Slurm configuration file. Each SlurmConfig gives its instance group a role, and the GPU group joins the train partition. AMI-based configuration sets up the nodes, and the security team’s compliance agent is already in the custom AMI. The GPU group asks for 8 nodes and requires 6, so jobs can start as soon as 6 are ready while HyperPod provisions the rest and health checks each GPU node as it arrives. HyperPod selects tree topology for the P5 nodes on its own, and accounting writes to an Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) database that the team owns. The administrator can still SSH into the controller node to inspect or tune Slurm directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="install-observability"&gt;Install observability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Leadership wants GPU and job metrics, so the administrator installs the observability extension. In the SageMaker AI console, on the cluster’s Extensions tab, the administrator chooses the observability extension, picks the metric categories and their levels and the collection interval, and selects one of two metric stores: Amazon CloudWatch or Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus. The following screenshot shows the install form with one store selected. The other option is available in the same field.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-7.png" alt="Console install form for the observability extension, with configurable metric levels and a choice of metric store" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Installing the observability extension from the console, with configurable metric levels and a choice of metric store&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same installation through the API takes one create-cluster-extension call. The following example uses Amazon CloudWatch as the metric store. To send metrics to Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus instead, you set the Prometheus destination in the same configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The administrator installs the extension with one create-cluster-extension call:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker create-cluster-extension \
    --cluster-arn arn:aws:sagemaker:us-west-2:{{111122223333}}:cluster/{{abcd1234efgh}} \
    --extension-name sagemaker-observability \
    --configuration-values file://observability-config.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The call returns the extension’s ARN, which the administrator uses in later calls:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "ExtensionArn": "arn:aws:sagemaker:us-west-2:111122223333:extension/sagemaker-abcd1234efgh/sagemaker-observability/0a1b2c3d4e5f"
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whichever way it’s installed, each node runs only the components relevant to its role, such as DCGM and EFA exporters on GPU nodes and Slurm cluster metrics on the controller. Schema errors are rejected before rollout, while node-specific conflicts appear in extension health.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the install, the extension appears on the cluster’s Extensions tab with its version, status, and ARN. The screenshot shows the tab for a cluster that has both the observability and user management extensions installed, and both are Active.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-8.png" alt="The Extensions tab for a cluster with both the observability and user management extensions installed and Active" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: The Extensions tab for a cluster with both the observability and user management extensions installed&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whichever metric store you choose, you can add an Amazon Managed Grafana workspace to the installation, so the team doesn’t need to build dashboards from scratch. After signing in to Grafana, they find two pre-created dashboards, Clusters and Jobs, as shown in the following screenshot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-9.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-9.png" alt="Pre-created Clusters and Jobs dashboards in Amazon Managed Grafana" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: The Clusters and Jobs dashboards that come with the observability extension in Amazon Managed Grafana&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Clusters dashboard gives a single view of your HyperPod Slurm clusters, built on the metrics that the extension collects and sends to your chosen metric store. At the top, it summarizes cluster health: instance count and health, GPU and GPU memory totals, vCPUs, memory, and Amazon FSx for Lustre capacity. It also shows the state of Slurm itself, including node states (idle, allocated, mixed, down, and draining), job queues, failed or preempted jobs, and scheduler activity. From there, you can filter by cluster, node, or GPU to drill into EFA networking, GPU performance and errors, and host-level CPU, memory, disk, and network metrics, as shown in the following screenshots. That lets you go from “is my cluster healthy?” to the root cause of an issue without leaving the dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-10.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-10.png" alt="Clusters dashboard summary of cluster health, down to per-node GPU, EFA, and host metrics" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 10: The Clusters dashboard, from cluster-wide health and Slurm state down to per-node GPU, EFA, and host metrics&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Jobs dashboard moves from cluster-level health down to individual Slurm jobs. It ties each job’s resource to use back to the job itself, using metrics from the Slurm job exporter in either metric store. You can filter by cluster, job ID, job name, partition, user, or account, and see CPU, memory, GPU utilization, GPU memory, and disk I/O for each job, plus accelerator and NCCL metrics. That makes it easier to spot underused GPUs, stuck jobs, or communication bottlenecks in distributed training, as in the following screenshot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-11.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-11.png" alt="Jobs dashboard showing resource use for individual Slurm jobs" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 11: The Jobs dashboard, showing resource use for individual Slurm jobs&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="turn-on-deeper-gpu-and-nccl-metrics"&gt;Turn on deeper GPU and NCCL metrics&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Later, a research team that is tuning multi-node training asks for more detail, so the administrator sends only the settings to change:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker update-cluster-extension \
    --cluster-arn {{cluster-arn}} \
    --extension-arn {{observability-extension-arn}} \
    --configuration-values '{"metricsProvider":{"acceleratedComputeMetrics":{"level":"ADVANCED"},"ncclMetrics":{"level":"ENABLED"}}}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;HyperPod merges these settings into the stored configuration and applies them to every node, restarting only the components whose settings changed. When the investigation is over, setting both to null returns them to their defaults, and at no point are nodes replaced or scripts rerun.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="fix-a-metrics-delivery-problem"&gt;Fix a metrics delivery problem&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One morning the extension shows as Degraded. A new instance group has an execution role that lacks aps:RemoteWrite, and describe-cluster-extension shows the effect:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "NodeSummary": { "TotalCount": 19, "ActiveCount": 11 },
    "Health": {
        "Issues": [
            {
                "Code": "ExtensionUnhealthy",
                "Message": "otel-exporter-health: no successful exports in 5m6s",
                "NodeCount": 8
            }
        ]
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once the administrator adds the permission, the nodes report healthy and the extension returns to Active, with nothing to reinstall.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="give-people-access"&gt;Give people access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The user management extension manages the Linux users and groups on every node, and who can sign in and use sudo on each node type. You declare who should exist and what they can do, and HyperPod applies it to every node, including nodes added later or replaced, so no one connects to nodes or runs scripts to add or remove a user. Users come from local accounts that you list in the configuration, from Active Directory over LDAPS, or from both, and each group names the node types (controller, login, or compute) where its members can sign in over SSH and where they get passwordless sudo.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the platform team, security wants corporate identities, with sudo reserved for administrators, and a partner lab that isn’t in the directory needs accounts of its own. The administrator declares both sources in one configuration, with group rules that say which node types each group can sign in to and use sudo on:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "sharedFilesystemMount": "/fsx",
    "userSources": {
        "localUsers": {
            "groups": [
                { "name": "cluster-admins", "gid": 3002,
                  "ssh": [ { "nodeTypes": ["controller", "login", "compute"] } ],
                  "sudo": [ { "nodeTypes": ["controller", "login", "compute"] } ] },
                { "name": "partner-lab", "gid": 3101,
                  "ssh": [ { "nodeTypes": ["login", "compute"] } ] }
            ],
            "users": [
                { "username": "admin-1", "uid": 2001, "groups": ["cluster-admins"] },
                { "username": "partner-1", "uid": 2101, "groups": ["partner-lab"] }
            ]
        },
        "ldap": {
            "ldapUri": ["ldaps://{{ad.example.com}}"],
            "ldapSearchBase": "{{dc=example,dc=com}}",
            "ldapDefaultBindDn": "{{CN=ReadOnly,OU=Users,DC=example,DC=com}}",
            "ldapDefaultAuthtokSecretArn": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-west-2:{{111122223333}}:secret:{{ad-readonly-AbCdEf}}",
            "ldapTlsCacertSecretArn": "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-west-2:{{111122223333}}:secret:{{ldaps-ca-GhIjKl}}",
            "domain": "{{example.com}}",
            "useFullyQualifiedNames": "ENABLED",
            "ldapGroups": [
                { "name": "{{ClusterAdmin@example.com}}",
                  "ssh": [ { "nodeTypes": ["controller", "login", "compute"] } ],
                  "sudo": [ { "nodeTypes": ["controller", "login", "compute"] } ] },
                { "name": "{{ml-researchers@example.com}}",
                  "ssh": [ { "nodeTypes": ["login", "compute"] } ] }
            ]
        }
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Access is explicit, so after the extension is installed only the groups that grant SSH access, along with the default ubuntu user, can sign in. Directory users keep their identities and group membership in Active Directory, while the partner lab’s users get the same IDs, home directories, and the default Slurm root account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same configuration can be managed from the console. Choosing Manage users on the user management extension opens the cluster’s Users tab, where the administrator can add, edit, and remove groups and users, and set which node types each group can sign in to and use sudo on, as shown in the following screenshot.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_145960" style="width: 2736px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-145960" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-145960" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ml-22428-figure-12.png" alt="" width="2726" height="1166"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-145960" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 12: Managing groups and users for the user management extension on the console&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="upgrade-an-extension-to-get-new-features-without-ami-updates"&gt;Upgrade an extension to get new features without AMI updates&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When AWS publishes a new version of an extension, the administrator lists the versions that can be installed and upgrades in place without AMI updates:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker list-cluster-extension-versions \
    --extension-name sagemaker-observability

aws sagemaker update-cluster-extension \
    --cluster-arn {{cluster-arn}} \
    --extension-arn {{observability-extension-arn}} \
    --extension-version {{new-version}}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;HyperPod checks the stored settings against the new version’s schema and rejects the request, changing nothing, if they aren’t valid for it. Otherwise, the new version rolls out to every node, and nodes that join later get it too. Upgrades only go forward, so the administrator picks a maintenance window. Any new settings or capabilities that come with the version can then be turned on with an update like the earlier one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scale-the-cluster-and-replace-failed-nodes"&gt;Scale the cluster and replace failed nodes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before the end-of-quarter run, the administrator starts an on-demand deep health check on the GPU group and then adds nodes with batch-add-cluster-nodes. The request returns as soon as HyperPod accepts it, so the administrator follows it as a cluster operation:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker list-cluster-operations \
    --cluster-name shared-gpu-cluster \
    --status InProgress&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As capacity arrives, each node is health checked, joins Slurm, takes its place in the topology, and gets both extensions with the same version and settings as every other node. If a GPU fails during the run, the replacement is configured and picks up the extensions the same way.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="review-who-has-access"&gt;Review who has access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the end of the quarter, security asks who has sudo on the controller, and the administrator answers from the API without signing in to a node:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker describe-cluster-extension \
    --cluster-arn {{cluster-arn}} \
    --extension-arn {{user-management-extension-arn}} \
    --query ExtensionDetails.ConfigurationValues --output text |
jq '[.userSources.localUsers.groups[]?, .userSources.ldap.ldapGroups[]?]
    | map(select(any(.sudo[]?; .nodeTypes | index("controller")))) | map(.name)'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The answer is cluster-admins and ClusterAdmin@example.com, and the directory group’s membership is audited in the directory. Because each extension has its own ARN, the platform team’s role can update only sagemaker-user-management and the monitoring team’s role only sagemaker-observability, while security has read-only access to both. None of these permissions belongs on the cluster’s execution role. When an extension is Active, ActiveCount equals TotalCount, and Health is empty, every node runs exactly that configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="availability-and-pricing"&gt;Availability and pricing&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cluster extensions, including the observability and user management extensions, have been available since November 2026 in all the regions where Amazon SageMaker HyperPod is supported, and the other improvements in this post were announced earlier in 2026, with each announcement listing its Regions. There’s no additional charge for cluster extensions. You pay for the AWS resources that you use with them, such as Amazon CloudWatch, Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus, and AWS Secrets Manager.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-13.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22428-13.png" alt="Summary of the two extensions available since November 2026. Observability sends node, accelerator, network, cluster, and Slurm job metrics to Amazon CloudWatch or Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus with dashboards in Amazon Managed Grafana and configurable metric levels and intervals. User management provides the same Linux users and groups on every node, control over who can sign in with SSH and use sudo, and users from a local list or an LDAP directory" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 13: The two extensions that have been available since November 2026&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A cluster is easier to run when more of it is declared, validated, converged, and observable. This year’s improvements brought that to a HyperPod Slurm cluster’s definition, node setup, capacity, network topology, and hardware health, and cluster extensions, available since November 2026, bring it to the software on the nodes, the layer that changes most often. Lifecycle scripts and custom AMIs stay focused on what’s specific to your organization, and the controller node stays yours.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To create a cluster as described in this post, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/smcluster-getting-started-slurm-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with SageMaker HyperPod using the AWS CLI&lt;/a&gt;. To get started with cluster extensions, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-slurm-extensions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cluster Extensions in the Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;. Share your feedback in the comments or through your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="acknowledgment"&gt;Acknowledgment&lt;/h3&gt;

Some of the people involved in developing this include Hengyu Lou, Aman Pratap Singh, Karthick Ravi, Shubh Nisar, Nitish Sai Kommasai, Uday Singamala, Min Badar, Devanshi Desai, Yahya Hassan, Connie Chen, Kashish Tayal, Oleg Talalov, Takuma Yoshitami, Kiran Penta, Dhawal Parkar, Pradeep Cruz.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Introducing Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Global Inference Gateway: One endpoint across Regions and clusters</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-global-inference-gateway-one-endpoint-across-regions-and-clusters/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dmitry Soldatkin]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 20 Nov 2026 22:35:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">7d1155e7ae64ab335a37cde382657a457dce3267</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker HyperPod introduces the Global Inference Gateway, one managed endpoint that routes inference requests across your HyperPod clusters in one Region or across Regions. It picks the best cluster, fails over automatically, and authenticates every request with SigV4 and IAM over AWS PrivateLink.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;During a traffic peak, a request can sit in a queue on a saturated HyperPod cluster while GPUs on another cluster, sometimes in another AWS Region, sit idle. You have already paid for that capacity, but your requests can’t reach it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introducing Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, we showed how the Inference Gateway gives each Amazon SageMaker HyperPod cluster a single, GPU-aware endpoint. It reads real-time signals such as key-value (KV) cache utilization and queue depth to place every request on the best-suited pod. That solves routing inside one cluster, but not across clusters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, SageMaker HyperPod introduces the Global Inference Gateway. It is one SageMaker-managed gateway that routes across every cluster you register, in one Region or across Regions. The gateway picks the cluster and carries the request over the AWS backbone, with automatic failover and authentication through AWS Signature Version 4 (SigV4) and AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM). Inside each cluster, the Inference Gateway keeps picking the pod, unchanged. The Global Inference Gateway is the second tier of the architecture we described in part 1.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-capacity-spread-across-clusters-and-regions"&gt;The challenge: Capacity spread across clusters and Regions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Models are getting larger, and the GPU capacity to serve them is harder to find. To get enough of it, you spread it across more clusters and more Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider a team that serves four models across five HyperPod clusters in two Regions. Without the Inference Gateway, clients track 20 endpoints: one per model per cluster. The Inference Gateway cuts that to five, one per cluster. But each client still picks a cluster itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That choice has costs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Uneven load.&lt;/strong&gt; During peaks, requests queue in one Region while GPUs in the other Region are idle.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Manual failover.&lt;/strong&gt; When a cluster fails, an engineer changes configuration to move traffic away from it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom plumbing.&lt;/strong&gt; Spreading traffic across Regions means building and maintaining load balancers, DNS, and cross-Region networking yourself.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team needs one endpoint that sends each request to the best-suited cluster and moves traffic off a failed cluster on its own.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-benefits"&gt;Key benefits&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Global Inference Gateway helps you get more from the GPU capacity you already have, with less to operate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Put idle GPUs to work.&lt;/strong&gt; During a peak, requests overflow to clusters with available capacity in the same Region or another one, instead of queuing on a saturated cluster.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fail over automatically.&lt;/strong&gt; When a cluster fails, the gateway moves its traffic to the next-best cluster. Clients don’t change anything.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use one endpoint for everything.&lt;/strong&gt; Clients call a single SageMaker-managed endpoint instead of tracking one endpoint per cluster or per model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skip the custom networking.&lt;/strong&gt; You don’t build or maintain load balancers, DNS, or cross-Region networking.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stay private by default.&lt;/strong&gt; Every request is authenticated with SigV4 and IAM, and it reaches your clusters only over AWS PrivateLink.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="customer-feedback"&gt;Customer feedback&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“At Bagelcode, we run our own LLM inference service on AWS to help teams across the company, including our game teams, use AI to improve how they work. In our beta tests, the Global Inference Gateway in Amazon SageMaker HyperPod routed requests across two AWS Regions through a single endpoint and kept serving them when one cluster could no longer serve the model. That gave our small platform team cross-Region failover without having to build or maintain our own routing layer.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Jinhyung Park, VP (AI Platform), Bagelcode&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Setting up the gateway takes two decisions: which clusters to open up for routing, and which of them to group behind one gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Publish a cluster.&lt;/strong&gt; You do this once per cluster. You turn on the &lt;code&gt;spec.globalRouting&lt;/code&gt; block in the cluster’s existing &lt;code&gt;InferenceGatewayConfig&lt;/code&gt;. The HyperPod Inference controller then publishes the cluster privately from your own account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create the gateway.&lt;/strong&gt; You do this once per gateway. You call &lt;code&gt;CreateGlobalInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt; with the clusters to route across, and SageMaker returns one endpoint.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A cluster is reachable only after you publish it. When you publish a cluster, the controller creates a Network Load Balancer in front of the cluster’s Inference Gateway, publishes a virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoint service on it, and uploads the listener’s certificate to your TLS bucket. These resources live in your account. If you list a cluster in a gateway before you publish it, the call fails with a message that tells you what to fix.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-a-request-flows"&gt;How a request flows&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the path of each request: (1) your client signs the request with SigV4 and sends it to the gateway’s regional endpoint. (2) The gateway authenticates the caller and picks the best cluster. (3) AWS PrivateLink carries the request to that cluster, in the same Region or another, and (4) the cluster’s Inference Gateway routes it to a model pod, as it does today.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22376-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/ML-22376-1-1.png" alt="Figure 1: Global Inference Gateway architecture" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The Global Inference Gateway receives each request at one regional endpoint and forwards it over AWS PrivateLink to the best HyperPod cluster, where the cluster’s Inference Gateway routes it to a model pod. Each cluster publishes its load back to the gateway.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="what-aws-operates"&gt;What AWS operates&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You decide which clusters to publish, which to group behind a gateway, and how to weight routing. AWS operates the rest:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The routing fleet.&lt;/strong&gt; SageMaker provisions and operates the fleet that terminates TLS, authenticates callers, scores clusters, and forwards each request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private connectivity.&lt;/strong&gt; SageMaker creates one private endpoint per cluster and carries traffic on the AWS backbone, in-Region and cross-Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Certificates and validation.&lt;/strong&gt; Using an execution role that you create, the service reads each cluster’s VPC endpoint service name, certificate, and Region, so you don’t copy them by hand. It checks them before routing traffic.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Health and failover.&lt;/strong&gt; The gateway health-checks every cluster and moves traffic off a failed cluster automatically. For details, see the Operating the gateway section.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authentication and rate limits.&lt;/strong&gt; The gateway checks SigV4 signatures, IAM permissions, and rate limits, and rejects failing requests before any cluster sees them.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On each cluster, a service-managed metrics publisher with its own IAM role sends the cluster’s load to the gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-the-gateway-picks-a-cluster"&gt;How the gateway picks a cluster&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For each request, the gateway scores every healthy cluster on four signals and sends the request to the highest-scoring cluster. Each cluster’s metrics publisher supplies the load signals.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You control how much each signal counts with routing weights. Each weight is a value from 0.0 to 1.0, and a weight of 0 turns that signal off. If you set weights, you set all four, and they must add up to 1.0. If you omit them, the defaults in the following table apply.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Weight&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it scores&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Default&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;KVHeadroom&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Available KV cache and GPU memory on the cluster&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.20&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;QueueDepth&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;How backed up the cluster is&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.25&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;RegionProximity&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Distance between the cluster and the gateway’s Region&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.40&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ErrorRate&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Recent failed requests on the cluster&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0.15&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The defaults favor clusters close to the gateway’s Region, while still moving traffic toward clusters with available capacity and away from clusters that are failing. If resilience matters more than proximity for your workload, raise &lt;code&gt;ErrorRate&lt;/code&gt; and lower &lt;code&gt;RegionProximity&lt;/code&gt;. For example, the following routing block weights recent errors at 0.7:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{"Weights": {"KVHeadroom": 0.1, "QueueDepth": 0.1, "RegionProximity": 0.1, "ErrorRate": 0.7}}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You pass the weights in the &lt;code&gt;Routing&lt;/code&gt; parameter of &lt;code&gt;CreateGlobalInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt;, and you can change them later with &lt;code&gt;UpdateGlobalInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choose-your-starting-point"&gt;Choose your starting point&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your path depends on what you run today. If you serve from a single cluster, stay on that cluster’s Inference Gateway endpoint. Move to the Global Inference Gateway when you add a second cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Starting point&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you do&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The Inference Gateway runs on each cluster, for example four models on five clusters in us-east-1 and us-west-2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Update the add-on, publish each cluster, and create the gateway&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One endpoint for every cluster. Per-cluster endpoints keep serving while you move clients over.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The HyperPod Inference add-on runs with the Inference Gateway turned off&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Turn on the Inference Gateway with one add-on update and one &lt;code&gt;InferenceGatewayConfig&lt;/code&gt; per cluster, add a scheduler for each model, then follow the first row&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-model endpoints collapse into one&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GPU capacity in two Regions, but no HyperPod Inference setup yet&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create a HyperPod cluster in each Region with the HyperPod Inference add-on, deploy your model, turn on the Inference Gateway, then follow the first row&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;One endpoint from day one, with traffic whose routing can be fine-tuned, and keeps serving if a Region has a problem&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To set up the Global Inference Gateway, you prepare each cluster, create an execution role, and call &lt;code&gt;CreateGlobalInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt;. You then send OpenAI-compatible requests, signed with SigV4, to the gateway endpoint. For step-by-step instructions, including the IAM roles and policies, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-global-inference-gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-access"&gt;Security and access&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every request to the gateway is signed with SigV4 and authorized with IAM. Callers need permission to invoke the gateway, and streaming has its own action:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": [
      "sagemaker:InvokeGlobalInferenceGateway",
      "sagemaker:InvokeGlobalInferenceGatewayWithResponseStream"
    ],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:sagemaker:&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;account-id&amp;gt;:global-inference-gateway/&amp;lt;gateway-id&amp;gt;"
  }]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Attach this policy to each IAM identity that sends requests. To allow every gateway in the account, use &lt;code&gt;global-inference-gateway/*&lt;/code&gt; as the resource.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The execution role follows least privilege. It can describe only the clusters you list, reach only their Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) API, and read only the routing certificates under the &lt;code&gt;gig/&lt;/code&gt; prefix of your TLS bucket, tagged as created by HyperPod Inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity tokens.&lt;/strong&gt; If your clusters check JSON Web Tokens (JWTs), pass the token with &lt;code&gt;--customer-identity-token&lt;/code&gt; (the &lt;code&gt;X-Amzn-SageMaker-Customer-Identity-Token&lt;/code&gt; header), with no &lt;code&gt;Bearer&lt;/code&gt; prefix. The token travels inside the SigV4 signature, so an unsigned token is rejected with 403 and a repeated token with 400. Turn on JWT checks on every cluster that serves a model, or on none of them, so a request doesn’t succeed on one cluster and fail on another.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Requests reach your clusters only through AWS PrivateLink, as shown in Figure 1. The gateway does not log or store prompts or completions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="operating-the-gateway"&gt;Operating the gateway&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Here are steps to configure the gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="add-a-cluster"&gt;Add a cluster&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prepare the new cluster and create the execution role’s EKS access entry on it, as described in the documentation. Also add the new cluster to the &lt;code&gt;Resource&lt;/code&gt; lists of the execution role’s permissions policy. Then call &lt;code&gt;AddInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt; in the gateway’s Region, even if the new cluster is in another Region:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker add-inference-gateway \
  --identifier &amp;lt;gateway-name&amp;gt; \
  --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt; \
  --cluster-arn arn:aws:sagemaker:&amp;lt;region-3&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;account-id&amp;gt;:cluster/&amp;lt;cluster-id-3&amp;gt; \
  --name &amp;lt;gateway-config-name-3&amp;gt; \
  --namespace inference-gateway&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway validates the cluster and starts routing to it once it is healthy. The endpoint, other clusters, and clients aren’t affected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="detach-a-cluster"&gt;Detach a cluster&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Call &lt;code&gt;RemoveInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt; in the gateway’s Region:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker remove-inference-gateway \
  --identifier &amp;lt;gateway-name&amp;gt; \
  --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt; \
  --cluster-arn arn:aws:sagemaker:&amp;lt;region-3&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;account-id&amp;gt;:cluster/&amp;lt;cluster-id-3&amp;gt; \
  --name &amp;lt;gateway-config-name-3&amp;gt; \
  --namespace inference-gateway&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway stops sending new requests to the cluster, lets in-flight requests finish, and removes the private endpoint. A gateway always keeps at least one cluster. To withdraw the cluster fully, set &lt;code&gt;globalRouting.enabled: false&lt;/code&gt; in its &lt;code&gt;InferenceGatewayConfig&lt;/code&gt; and reapply it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="delete-the-gateway"&gt;Delete the gateway&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Call &lt;code&gt;DeleteGlobalInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker delete-global-inference-gateway \
  --identifier &amp;lt;gateway-name&amp;gt; --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway endpoint stops serving, and per-cluster endpoints keep serving. After the gateway is deleted, withdraw each cluster with &lt;code&gt;globalRouting.enabled: false&lt;/code&gt;, then delete the execution role and the IAM permissions you added for global routing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes how common events and changes affect traffic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Event&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What happens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What you do&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gateway creation fails&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The gateway moves to &lt;code&gt;FAILED&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;FailureReason&lt;/code&gt; says what failed, such as a certificate name mismatch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fix the cause. To see each cluster’s status, call &lt;code&gt;ListInferenceGateways&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A cluster fails&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Traffic moves to the next-best cluster, and the cluster returns gradually after it recovers. A failed request is retried once on another cluster unless it has started streaming. If no healthy cluster is left, the gateway returns 503.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Nothing. Clients don’t change anything.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Change routing weights&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;New requests use the new weights, and in-flight requests finish under the old ones. Nothing is re-provisioned.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Call &lt;code&gt;UpdateGlobalInferenceGateway&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Add a cluster&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Routing starts once the cluster is healthy. Nothing else changes.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;See Add a cluster&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Detach a cluster&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;New requests stop, and in-flight requests finish&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;See Detach a cluster&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Delete the gateway&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The gateway endpoint stops serving, and per-cluster endpoints keep serving&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;See Delete the gateway&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For any troubleshooting assistance, please see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-ts-global-inference-gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Troubleshooting Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-and-considerations"&gt;Best practices and considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Plan your groups before you publish.&lt;/strong&gt; Decide which clusters sit behind each gateway, with the same &lt;code&gt;modelName&lt;/code&gt; on every cluster that should share a model’s traffic. If you want peak traffic to overflow across Regions, give clusters in both Regions headroom.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Publish only the clusters you add to a gateway.&lt;/strong&gt; A published cluster that isn’t in any gateway receives no traffic, but its Network Load Balancer still runs and is billed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cover every model.&lt;/strong&gt; Each scheduler in the &lt;code&gt;InferenceGatewayConfig&lt;/code&gt; maps a model name to the pods that serve it. A model that isn’t in any scheduler can’t be reached through the gateway, and requests for it return a 404 error.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep your add-on configuration complete.&lt;/strong&gt; With &lt;code&gt;--resolve-conflicts OVERWRITE&lt;/code&gt;, an incomplete configuration can reset your load balancer or TLS settings. Start from the current configuration every time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pass&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;--context&lt;/code&gt; &lt;strong&gt;on every&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt; &lt;strong&gt;command.&lt;/strong&gt; You work across several clusters, so make sure each command runs against the cluster you mean.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep a workload in one Region when you need to.&lt;/strong&gt; Create a gateway that includes only clusters in that Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we introduced the Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Global Inference Gateway: one SageMaker-managed endpoint across your HyperPod clusters, in one Region or several. The gateway picks the cluster based on KV cache headroom, queue depth, Region proximity, and error rate, and moves traffic off a failed cluster with no client changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Global Inference Gateway is available in 13 AWS Regions: US East (N. Virginia), US East (Ohio), US West (N. California), US West (Oregon), South America (São Paulo), Europe (Frankfurt), Europe (Ireland), Europe (London), Europe (Stockholm), Asia Pacific (Tokyo), Asia Pacific (Mumbai), Asia Pacific (Singapore), and Asia Pacific (Sydney).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-global-inference-gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
