			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Standard Bank Group automates AI risk assessment for generative AI</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-standard-bank-group-automates-ai-risk-assessment-for-generative-ai/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Tala Qraitem]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 20 Nov 2026 20:37:31 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Thought Leadership]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">c9f8f02479f8aa3b6d8d4df189a3bdad61de5a89</guid>

					<description>Standard Bank Group automated the evidence behind its generative AI approval gates. Working with AWS Professional Services, the bank built an assessment in its own AWS account that scores each use case against international AI standards and its own policies using LLM-as-a-judge on Amazon Bedrock, giving risk committees consistent, traceable evidence.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Tala Qraitem from AWS and Jörg Fischer from Standard Bank Group&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Having an AI policy is one thing. Proving, one use case at a time, that AI systems follow it is another. Every new generative AI application adds one more system to prove. Teams can now build one in days, but each still needs a risk committee’s approval before it reaches customers or employees. When that approval depends on reviewers reading documents and testing the system by hand, governance becomes the slowest step in the pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Standard Bank Group chose to automate that proof. Working with AWS Professional Services, the bank deployed an automated assessment that scores each generative AI use case against international AI standards and its own policies. The assessment produces reports and a dashboard that show what is already in place, what is working well, and what still needs mitigation. This post explains how the assessment runs on AWS and what Standard Bank Group learned from its first runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="governing-ai-with-the-framework-you-already-have"&gt;Governing AI with the framework you already have&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Standard Bank Group didn’t need a new AI policy. Like most large banks, it already had an established model risk practice that covers concerns such as explainability and fairness, and a change risk process that assesses each new use case according to its level of risk before it goes live.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Generative AI increases the complexity, because models and tooling change every three to six months, yet each use case still needs to meet the same high security and compliance bar.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“At Standard Bank, this assessment demonstrates how we are working with AWS to embed intelligent, automated risk management into our AI journey, allowing us to accelerate meaningful business value while maintaining the trust and controls that banking demands. We believe responsible AI is not about slowing innovation down but enabling it to build and scale with confidence.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Jörg Fischer, Group Chief Information and AI Officer, Standard Bank Group&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The goal was to automate evidence gathering and testing, then feed the results into the approval gates Standard Bank Group already runs. Review and approval stay with the risk committee, but the committee now bases each decision on consistent evidence, collected the same way across use cases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-the-assessment-works"&gt;How the assessment works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows how the assessment’s components fit together in Standard Bank Group’s AWS account, from intake through evaluation to reporting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22493-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22493-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of the automated AI risk assessment running in Standard Bank Group’s AWS account" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The assessment pipeline in Standard Bank Group’s AWS account, from intake through evaluation to reporting&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The assessment runs entirely in Standard Bank Group’s own AWS account. Documents, code, and test results stay in an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket there. The AWS Well-Architected Tool handles intake, AWS Step Functions orchestrates the pipeline, and a foundation model on Amazon Bedrock evaluates the evidence. It uses a technique called LLM-as-a-judge, in which a large language model checks evidence against defined criteria.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Amazon Bedrock, Standard Bank Group could use a foundation model without stepping outside the controls it already runs on AWS. The bank controls access to the model through AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), just as it does for its other AWS workloads. Amazon Bedrock doesn’t share the bank’s prompts or model outputs with model providers, and doesn’t use them to train models. Because Amazon Bedrock offers many foundation models through one API, the team can move to a newer judge model by changing a configuration setting instead of rebuilding the pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each assessment moves through four steps. In the first two, a pre-assessment agent collects evidence about the use case, then the pipeline examines it and tests the application directly. The third evaluates that evidence against controls. The fourth summarizes the results in a standard, predefined format and delivers them to the people who act on them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-a-pre-assessment-agent-collects-the-evidence"&gt;Step 1: A pre-assessment agent collects the evidence&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The custom lens in the Well-Architected Tool holds about 40 questions, each covering a risk event such as incomplete technical documentation or insufficient human oversight. Delivery teams no longer fill it in from scratch. A pre-assessment agent running on AWS Fargate scans the use case’s code repository and documentation. It maps relevant files to each question and pre-populates the lens with answers and links to the evidence. The delivery team then reviews those answers, corrects any mistakes, and adds the evidence the agent couldn’t find.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-the-pipeline-examines-the-artifacts-and-tests-the-application"&gt;Step 2: The pipeline examines the artifacts and tests the application&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pipeline works from four kinds of evidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;System documentation, which describes how the application was designed, built, and evaluated.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Policy documents, which set out roles, responsibilities, and procedures such as incident handling.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Source code and the system prompt, which show how controls are implemented.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Live calls to the application, which show how it behaves in practice.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For documents and code, the pipeline checks whether each artifact contains what its mapped control requires. Each control comes from an international AI standard or framework, or from Standard Bank Group’s own policies. An incident procedure, for example, needs to describe how incidents are reported.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For live testing, the pipeline calls the application through its AWS Lambda function or API. The delivery team supplies questions a typical user would ask. The pipeline adds a built-in set of jailbreak attempts and off-topic questions, which the application is expected to decline. It also sends the same prompt several times, in its original and reworded forms, and uses an embedding model on Amazon Bedrock to check that the answers stay consistent in meaning. Where the team provides expected answers, the pipeline measures how closely the responses match them. Every response is stored in Amazon S3, so reviewers can see exactly what the application generated.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-llm-as-a-judge-evaluation-against-standards"&gt;Step 3: LLM-as-a-judge evaluation against standards&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this step, a foundation model on Amazon Bedrock acts as the judge. The assessment uses the model as-is with no fine-tuning or customization. (Model availability varies by AWS Region, so check the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supported models by Region&lt;/a&gt; before you deploy.) For each question, the model checks whether specific evidence appears in the artifacts and in the application’s responses, and returns yes or no.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every check maps to a control in an international standard or framework for AI management, AI risk, or AI security. Standard Bank Group also customized the lens with judge questions built from its own standards, bringing the total to more than 300. The checks cover the eight AWS responsible AI dimensions in the following table:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dimension&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the assessment looks for&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Veracity and robustness&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Correct outputs, even with unexpected or adversarial inputs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Privacy and security&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Appropriate collection, use, and protection of data and models&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Transparency&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Information that helps stakeholders make informed choices about their use of an AI system&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fairness&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Consideration of impacts on different groups of stakeholders&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Safety&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Prevention of harmful output and misuse&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Controllability&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mechanisms to monitor and steer system behavior&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Governance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Best practices across the AI supply chain, including providers and deployers&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Explainability&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Ways to understand and evaluate system outputs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each risk receives a likelihood score, the share of required evidence the model couldn’t find, and a fixed severity level that the bank’s risk team can adjust. Together they produce a rating from Very low to Critical. Each missing piece of evidence maps to a specific mitigation and to the clause of the standard or policy it came from. A risk officer can then trace any findings back to their source when explaining decisions to a regulator. Figure 2 follows one missing piece of evidence through this process, from the evidence request to the recommended fix.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22493-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22493-2.png" alt="A single missing piece of evidence traced to a scored finding and a recommended fix" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: How one finding is traced from evidence to fix&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Standard Bank Group protects and checks the assessment’s output in two ways. Every AI model interaction is protected by Amazon Bedrock Guardrails, ensuring alignment with the bank’s established security and responsible AI standards. The assessment’s model calls pass through the same safeguards the bank enforces across its AWS accounts for its AI workloads. These guardrails screen for harmful content and for attempts to manipulate the model. &lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The assessment is also designed so its output can be checked. Instead of asking the model for an open-ended opinion, the pipeline asks narrow yes-or-no questions, each worded to match a specific control. A fixed formula that the risk team can inspect turns those answers into scores. Each evaluation runs multiple times to measure how consistent the answers are and any inconsistent findings are flagged in the review interface. Every judgment is stored with the evidence behind it so that reviewers and auditors can trace how it was reached.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-reports-for-each-audience"&gt;Step 4: Reports for each audience&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pipeline summarizes the results in a standard format and delivers them as an executive presentation, a mitigation checklist, a detailed technical report, and an Amazon CloudWatch dashboard that tracks scores across use cases over time. Before a gate decision, reviewers confirm findings in a custom review interface with single sign-on. There they can add the use case, review the questions, amend answers with a business justification, and rewrite the output.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The assessment produces evidence against controls. It doesn’t certify compliance with any regulation, and Standard Bank Group remains responsible for its own regulatory determinations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="testing-against-standard-bank-groups-own-rules"&gt;Testing against Standard Bank Group’s own rules&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;International standards give every use case a common baseline, but the bank’s risk committees approve use cases against its own policy. A question factory addresses this by generating extra judge questions from internal documents. Standard Bank Group’s risk team used it to turn internal policies into checks that run on every use case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because those checks are derived directly from policy language, a policy change becomes a test change. Every subsequent assessment applies the updated rule automatically, with minimal manual rework by the risk team.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Most organizations treat policy as something you read. Standard Bank Group treats it as something you run. When every rule becomes a check that runs against the live system, a policy update stops being a memo and becomes a test. That fundamentally changes how fast a bank can scale AI responsibly.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Sri Elaprolu, Director, Frontier AI Science &amp;amp; Engineering, AWS&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="built-into-the-use-case-lifecycle"&gt;Built into the use case lifecycle&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The assessment fits into three stages of the use case lifecycle. During a proof of concept, it is optional, giving teams an early view of potential gaps. At the build stage, it becomes mandatory, and the approval gate draws on its results as formal evidence. Today the assessment runs in Standard Bank Group’s production environment, evaluating use cases before they go live. After live workloads are connected, the assessment will rerun on a schedule or whenever code or configuration changes, keeping the evidence current well beyond launch. The bank’s teams already trigger assessments on their own through a central system where use cases flow from intake to evaluation in a single workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-this-means-for-financial-institutions"&gt;What this means for financial institutions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At Standard Bank Group, the assessment reduced a traditionally manual two-week review to a comprehensive risk report generated in approximately two hours, demonstrated across six assessments. It also delivered a 14% improvement in use-case risk scores after remediation. This strengthens responsible AI governance, reduces compliance bottlenecks, and shortens the path from assessment to production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This approach can benefit three groups involved in approving AI use cases:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Risk and compliance leaders get evidence in the same format for every use case, traceable to a published standard or an internal policy clause. Start from the framework you already run, as Standard Bank Group did with its existing risk processes and approval gates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delivery teams learn what to fix during the proof of concept, when changes cost the least, instead of meeting a rejection at the approval gate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Executives can grow the AI portfolio without growing the review effort at the same rate.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Adoption of AI at scale across the enterprise requires trust, both internally and with our customers. This assessment is proving key in enabling all our product teams to demonstrate that they are building enterprise-grade solutions aligned with both our own and industry best practices.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Duncan Macdonald, Chief Technology Officer, Standard Bank Group&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="from-approval-gates-to-continuous-assurance"&gt;From approval gates to continuous assurance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Banks designed approval gates for systems that changed a few times a year. Generative AI applications change with every prompt update, model version, and new data source. As they take on more autonomous tasks, the number of decisions that need evidence keeps growing. Adding reviewers can’t keep pace with that rate of change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Standard Bank Group’s approach points to a different operating model. When policies become tests and evidence is collected automatically, governance moves out of a checkpoint at the end of delivery and into the system itself. Every AI application in the portfolio can carry a current, auditable record of how it meets the bank’s standards, refreshed whenever its code or configuration changes. Risk teams spend their time on judgment calls and policy design, and the bank can show regulators the same evidence it uses for its own decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same foundation extends further. Continuous reassessment keeps evidence aligned with a changing code base after launch. New regulations can be encoded once as checks and applied across every use case. Aggregated scores give boards an enterprise-wide view of AI risk instead of a collection of individual reviews.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For any institution scaling AI, the question worth asking is how many of its policies have ever been tested against a running system. To explore how &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/professional-services/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Professional Services&lt;/a&gt; can help your organization automate AI risk assessment, contact your AWS account team. To learn more about building AI responsibly on AWS, visit &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/responsible-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Responsible AI on AWS&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Announcing Amazon Textract Insights: Verifiable generative AI for documents</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/announcing-amazon-textract-insights-verifiable-generative-ai-for-documents/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Keith Mascarenhas]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 20 Nov 2026 20:22:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Textract]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">9fcc26a95aa36a1d89af71508fa651ff1a91c61f</guid>

					<description>Announcing the general availability of Amazon Textract Insights, a new generative AI feature of Amazon Textract. Describe the fields you need in natural language, submit a document or a mixed packet, and receive structured results with a confidence score and page-level grounding for each value, with no model training required.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Organizations across financial services, insurance, healthcare, and the public sector depend on document processing. They turn scanned files and paper forms into structured data their systems can act on. As generative AI has matured, these organizations want more than accurate text extraction. They want to describe the information they need in their own words and get dependable, auditable answers from complex documents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we are announcing the general availability of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/textract/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Textract Insights&lt;/a&gt;, a new generative AI feature of Amazon Textract available through a new API within Amazon Textract. With Amazon Textract Insights, you describe the fields you want in natural language and submit a document or a mixed, multi-document packet. You receive structured results in which each value carries a confidence score and page-level grounding, with no model training required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-of-extracting-data-from-mixed-packets"&gt;The challenge of extracting data from mixed packets&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many of the high-value document workloads involve diverse, mixed packets rather than clean, single-purpose files. A mortgage file, for example, typically arrives as a single scan holding a W-2, payslips, bank statements, and a page of handwritten notes. Each has its own layout and appears in no fixed order. Claims files and onboarding packages can look very similar: typed forms, dense tables, scanned receipts, and free-text letters interleaved in one file.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams usually take one of two approaches, and each has trade-offs. The first is a custom pipeline that combines optical character recognition, document splitting, classification, a foundation model prompt, and a review step. The team must integrate, scale, and operate all of it. The second is a general-purpose large language model used by default. It returns fluent answers but does not reliably cite the source page or give a confidence score. Without a traceable source and a calibrated score, it is hard to know which values to accept automatically and which to review. This uncertainty often limits how far teams can automate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Textract Insights is built for reliable document processing, bringing this entire workflow into a single asynchronous request on Amazon Textract. Rather than assembling and operating a custom pipeline, your team works through one managed API and moves directly to the data it needs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-amazon-textract-insights-works"&gt;How Amazon Textract Insights works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Amazon Textract Insights, you define what to extract in a blueprint. A blueprint is a JSON Schema in which each field carries a natural language description of what to find. Each field also has an inference type that determines whether the value is read verbatim from the page or inferred from the content of the document. You can begin with an AWS public blueprint for a common document type, such as a W-2, Form 1040, Form 1098, a passport, or a birth certificate. You can also author your own blueprint for the documents specific to your business.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a mixed packet, you add the blueprints for each document type you expect to the same project. You then submit a single file of up to 3,000 pages from your own Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) bucket and enable splitting. Amazon Textract Insights splits the packet into logical sub-documents, matches each one to the blueprint that fits it, extracts the requested fields, and returns a confidence score and grounding for each value.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, suppose you receive a mortgage application as a single 26-page PDF that contains a W-2, payslips, bank statements, and a loan officer’s handwritten notes. You create an Amazon Textract Insights project with the AWS public W-2, payslip, and bank statement blueprints, add a custom blueprint for loan officer notes, and enable splitting. You then submit the file from your Amazon S3 bucket with a single StartDocumentInsights call. Amazon Textract Insights splits the file into its individual documents, matches each one to its blueprint, and writes the results to your S3 bucket. Each value includes a confidence score and the page and bounding box it was drawn from.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="confidence-and-grounding-for-auditable-automation"&gt;Confidence and grounding for auditable automation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Textract Insights returns two kinds of fields: The first are explicit fields read directly from the page. The second are inferred fields derived from the document’s content. Both return with a confidence score and with grounding that identifies the page and the coordinates the value was drawn from.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because each value is traceable to its source, a reviewer or an auditor can confirm any result against the original document. You can extend automation to documents that would previously have gone to manual review, while keeping people in the process wherever judgment is required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“We evaluated Amazon Textract Insights and are excited about its potential. Our medical bills and forms are received from a wide variety of sources and often have different layouts and formats. As a result, extraction rules developed for one source do not always transfer effectively to another.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;With Amazon Textract Insights, we can describe the fields we need in plain language, and the service identifies and extracts the corresponding data. Each extracted value is returned with a confidence score, allowing our team to focus their review efforts only on the fields where the service has lower confidence. We are also excited to explore the document splitting capabilities, which could help automatically separate multi-document files into individual documents and further streamline our document processing workflow.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Vijay Agrawal, Vice President, Information Systems, Symbeo, A CorVel Company&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="built-on-the-amazon-textract-engine"&gt;Built on the Amazon Textract engine&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We updated &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/11/textract-text-detection-model/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Textract’s reading engine&lt;/a&gt;, Amazon Textract Core, improving accuracy, strengthening the handling of dense tables and handwriting, and expanding language coverage to 40 languages for the DetectDocumentText and AnalyzeDocument APIs. All these benefits are available to Amazon Textract customers today with no changes. Your Amazon Textract APIs remain unchanged.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Textract Core is now &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/11/textract-tokyo-zurich/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generally available in two additional AWS Regions&lt;/a&gt;, Asia Pacific (Tokyo) and Europe (Zurich), bringing in-region processing to a total of 18 AWS Regions. Amazon Textract Insights isn’t yet available in these AWS Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-data-handling"&gt;Security and data handling&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="projects-and-blueprints"&gt;Projects and blueprints&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Textract Insights encrypts projects and blueprints at rest with AWS owned keys. To use your own AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) customer managed key, specify it in EncryptionConfiguration when you call CreateProject or CreateBlueprint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="insight-analysis-jobs"&gt;Insight analysis jobs&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Textract Insights reads documents from, and writes results to, the Amazon S3 buckets specified in your StartDocumentInsights request. Intermediate job data is encrypted at rest with AWS owned keys. If you specify a customer managed key in the request’s EncryptionConfiguration, Amazon Textract Insights uses it to encrypt the job’s intermediate data. All intermediate data is transient and is deleted when the job completes. Your output bucket’s default encryption applies to the results written to your bucket when the job completes. A project’s key does not apply to its jobs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you disable your key or remove Amazon Textract’s access to it, Amazon Textract Insights can no longer use data encrypted under it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cross-region-inference"&gt;Cross-Region inference&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Textract Insights automatically selects the optimal AWS Region within your geography to process your inference requests. This maximizes available compute resources and model availability, and is designed to deliver a consistent customer experience. All data is transmitted encrypted across Amazon’s secure network. Amazon Textract Insights securely routes your inference requests to available compute resources within the geographic area where the request originated. For example, inference requests originating in the United States are processed within the United States. Amazon Textract Core APIs are unchanged and continue to process requests in the AWS Region you call. For the list of destination AWS Regions, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/general/latest/gr/textract.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Textract documentation guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started"&gt;Getting started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Getting started with Amazon Textract Insights takes four steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Define what you need:&lt;/strong&gt; Start from an AWS public blueprint or describe your own fields in natural language.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create a project:&lt;/strong&gt; Add your blueprints to it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Submit your documents:&lt;/strong&gt; Send a single document or a mixed packet from your Amazon S3 bucket with one API call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review the results:&lt;/strong&gt; Accept values that meet your confidence threshold and route the rest for review. Tune your threshold on a representative sample of your own documents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Textract Insights supports PDF, PNG, JPEG, and TIFF inputs and processes documents in English, French, German, Italian, Portuguese, and Spanish. It is priced per page rather than per token, with no minimum commitment. For current rates across Amazon Textract, see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/textract/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Textract pricing page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Intelligent document processing has long forced a choice between how far you can automate and how well you can audit the result. Amazon Textract Insights narrows that trade-off so your team can decide where to automate based on evidence rather than guesswork. Learn more about getting started with Amazon Textract Insights.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/textract/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Textract product page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/textract/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Textract Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt; to author a blueprint or start with an AWS blueprint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Register for &lt;a href="https://registration.awsevents.com/flow/awsevents/reinvent2026/event-catalog/page/eventCatalog?search=AIM216" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Accelerate intelligent document processing with Amazon Textract&lt;/a&gt; session at re:Invent.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Introducing enhanced infrastructure observability in Amazon SageMaker HyperPod</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-enhanced-observability-in-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-to-diagnose-gpu-training-issues/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mijanur Palash]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 20 Nov 2026 18:24:06 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">08fb4e238d4eba4747871a78311b05e8f79a2286</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker HyperPod now offers enhanced observability to help you diagnose GPU training issues faster. It brings together diagnostic and profiling capabilities for training workloads on both Amazon EKS and Slurm orchestrators, pinpointing issues across GPU, network, and host layers.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Large-scale model training is one of the most expensive workloads you can run on GPUs, and a single unhealthy accelerator can stall an entire distributed job. When training throughput drops, the hard part is rarely noticing that something is wrong. It is figuring out why and where. Teams often spend hours or days sifting through logs and disconnected dashboards to decide whether a slowdown came from a throttled GPU, a saturated network link, or a stalled data pipeline on the host.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we are announcing enhanced observability in Amazon SageMaker HyperPod, a new capability that helps you diagnose GPU training issues faster. It brings together diagnostic and profiling capabilities for training workloads that run on both Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) and Slurm orchestrators:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Diagnostic summary&lt;/strong&gt; uses Amazon CloudWatch’s DetectGroupOutliers API to compare each GPU in a training job on utilization, memory, temperature, power, and SM clock, and flags GPUs that deviate from their peers. Static diagnostic rules surface common failure patterns such as GPU hardware faults (XID errors, ECC memory errors), EFA throughput drops, thermal throttling, and overheating, so you catch degradation before it affects training.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-you-need-it"&gt;Why you need it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Distributed training is expensive, and small inefficiencies compound into significant wasted GPU hours. Until now, getting visibility into HyperPod infrastructure meant stitching together separate tools, and even then, the metrics told you that performance had degraded without telling you why.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enhanced observability closes three gaps. It isolates a single bad accelerator across hundreds or thousands of GPUs whether through XID errors, ECC memory faults, or thermal throttling, so you spend time on the fix instead of the search.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, these capabilities reduce troubleshooting time and help you keep expensive accelerators busy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each capability targets a different layer of a distributed training job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="default-and-advanced-metrics"&gt;Default and advanced metrics&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enhanced observability collects telemetry at two levels:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Default metrics&lt;/strong&gt; (enabled automatically) cover cluster health, CPU/disk/OS-level usage, GPU utilization and temperature, and per-job resource utilization. These metrics are collected every 30 seconds and sent to your chosen metric store with approximately 1–2% CPU overhead and 250–300 MB of memory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Advanced metrics&lt;/strong&gt; (opt-in) provide deeper visibility:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Full node exporter suite:&lt;/strong&gt; comprehensive host-level metrics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;All NVIDIA GPU DCGM and AWS Neuron metrics:&lt;/strong&gt; detailed accelerator telemetry including clock speeds, power, PCIe activity, and error indicators.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) metrics:&lt;/strong&gt; network interface throughput, drops, retransmissions, and errors.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Advanced metrics are optional because they increase the volume of telemetry sent to your metric store, which has a cost implication. Most customers running small jobs on small clusters may not need them, but they are essential for long-running training jobs on large clusters where patterns emerge over time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="metric-stores"&gt;Metric stores&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can choose between two metric stores:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/strong&gt; (new default) provides native monitoring through CloudWatch SageMaker Insights, so you can visualize metrics without leaving the AWS Management Console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus&lt;/strong&gt; for teams that standardize on Prometheus and Grafana for observability.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Previously, only Prometheus was supported. CloudWatch is now the default, offering lower pricing compared to Amazon Managed Service Prometheus and tighter integration with the SageMaker console.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="diagnostic-summary"&gt;Diagnostic summary&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The diagnostic summary uses two complementary approaches to surface issues across your HyperPod cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPU outlier detection&lt;/strong&gt; uses CloudWatch’s &lt;em&gt;DetectGroupOutliers&lt;/em&gt; API to compare GPUs within a training job across utilization, memory usage, temperature, power draw, and SM clock frequency. When a GPU deviates significantly from its peers, it appears as an outlier card in the diagnostic summary. This works for both EKS and Slurm jobs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Static diagnostic rules&lt;/strong&gt; evaluate known failure patterns and organize detected issues by type:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hardware:&lt;/strong&gt; GPU ECC memory errors, XID faults, accelerator failures.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Network:&lt;/strong&gt; EFA throughput degradation, packet drops, retransmissions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throttling:&lt;/strong&gt; GPU clock throttling because of heat, board, or reliability limits.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Overheating:&lt;/strong&gt; GPU temperatures exceeding configured limits.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each detected issue identifies the affected job, cluster, node, accelerator, and rank.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-to-get-started"&gt;How to get started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enhanced observability is delivered as an add-on for EKS-orchestrated clusters and as an extension for Slurm-orchestrated clusters, which means you control who can install or change it through AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-create-a-sagemaker-hyperpod-cluster"&gt;Step 1: Create a SageMaker HyperPod cluster&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you already have a cluster, you can use it. To create a new SageMaker HyperPod cluster, you can follow the &lt;a href="https://awslabs.github.io/ai-on-sagemaker-hyperpod/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;step-by-step outline&lt;/a&gt;. In general, the steps are as follows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the Amazon SageMaker AI console, choose &lt;strong&gt;HyperPod clusters&lt;/strong&gt; in the navigation pane.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create HyperPod&lt;/strong&gt; cluster and &lt;strong&gt;Orchestrated on Amazon EKS&lt;/strong&gt; or &lt;strong&gt;Orchestrated by Slurm&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Setup options&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Custom setup&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Name&lt;/strong&gt;, enter a name.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Instance recovery&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Automatic&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Submit&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the Create HyperPod cluster page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-1.png" alt="Create HyperPod cluster page on Amazon EKS with Custom setup selected" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Creating a HyperPod cluster on Amazon EKS with Custom setup&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-configure-hyperpod-observability"&gt;Step 2: Configure HyperPod observability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;Custom setup&lt;/strong&gt; workflow, expand the &lt;strong&gt;HyperPod observability&lt;/strong&gt; section:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enable HyperPod Observability&lt;/strong&gt; is checked by default. Uncheck only if you do not want observability.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observability metrics&lt;/strong&gt; show default metrics (cluster health, CPU/disk/OS, GPU utilization/temperature, per-job resources) plus optional advanced metrics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select advanced metrics as needed: Full node exporter suite, All NVIDIA GPU DCGM/Neuron metrics and EFA metrics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enable logging&lt;/strong&gt; to create CloudWatch log groups for containers and pod logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Metric store&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/strong&gt; (default) or &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Grafana workspace&lt;/strong&gt;, choose to create a new workspace, use an existing one, or select None.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure IAM roles or let the console create them automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows the configuration options for HyperPod observability in the custom setup path:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-2-1.png" alt="HyperPod observability section showing default and advanced metrics, metric store, and Grafana workspace options" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The HyperPod observability section showing default and advanced metrics, metric store (CloudWatch or Prometheus), and Grafana workspace&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; In Quick setup, HyperPod observability is enabled by default and cannot be unchecked. Custom setup gives you control over which metric categories and advanced metrics to enable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-install-the-observability-add-on-using-the-cli-alternative-eks"&gt;Step 3: Install the observability add-on using the CLI (alternative, EKS)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can install the HyperPod observability add-on (EKS) or extension (Slurm) from the AWS Management Console or through the API. Alternatively, you can install the add-on with the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following command creates the add-on, enables the basic metric categories and leaves the opt-in profiling capabilities off:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks create-addon \
    --cluster-name &amp;lt;cluster-name&amp;gt; \
    --addon-name amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-observability \
    --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt; \
    --configuration-values '{
        "metricsDestination": "cloudwatch",
        "metricsProvider": {
            "nodeMetrics": {"level": "BASIC", "scrapeInterval": 30},
            "acceleratedComputeMetrics": {"level": "BASIC", "scrapeInterval": 30}
        },
        "profilesProvider": {
            "hostProfiling": {"enabled": false}
        }
    }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Verify the add-on is ACTIVE:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks describe-addon \
    --cluster-name &amp;lt;cluster-name&amp;gt; \
    --addon-name amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-observability \
    --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt; \
    --query 'addon.status'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows the observability add-on on the cluster’s Add-ons tab.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-3.png" alt="Add-ons tab on a HyperPod EKS cluster showing the Amazon SageMaker HyperPod observability add-on as Active with Amazon CloudWatch as the metrics store" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The observability add-on on the cluster’s Add-ons tab, active and sending metrics to Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-enable-metrics-eks"&gt;Step 4: Enable metrics (EKS)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can enable or disable any metric category or profiling capability at any time. For an EKS cluster, update the add-on configuration with the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI). The following command turns on advanced network (EFA) metrics:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks update-addon \
    --cluster-name &amp;lt;cluster-name&amp;gt; \
    --addon-name amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-observability \
    --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt; \
    --configuration-values '{
        "metricsDestination": "cloudwatch",
        "metricsProvider": {
            "nodeMetrics": {"level": "BASIC", "scrapeInterval": 30},
            "acceleratedComputeMetrics": {"level": "BASIC", "scrapeInterval": 30},
            "networkMetrics": {"level": "ADVANCED", "scrapeInterval": 30}
        }
    }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From the console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Actions&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Edit&lt;/strong&gt; on the add-on card to make the same changes. The following figure shows the &lt;strong&gt;Actions&lt;/strong&gt; button.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-4-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-4-1.png" alt="Observability add-on Actions menu with Edit, Update version, Delete, and View in EKS options" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Use Actions → Edit to enable or disable metric categories and profiling&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="slurm-clusters-install-or-edit-the-observability-extension"&gt;Slurm clusters: install or edit the observability extension&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On Slurm-orchestrated clusters, HyperPod observability is a cluster &lt;strong&gt;extension&lt;/strong&gt;. From the cluster detail page, open the &lt;strong&gt;Extensions&lt;/strong&gt; tab. If the extension is installed, choose &lt;strong&gt;Actions&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Edit&lt;/strong&gt;. The following figure shows the observability extension on a Slurm cluster’s &lt;strong&gt;Extensions&lt;/strong&gt; tab.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-5.png" alt="Slurm cluster Extensions tab showing the HyperPod observability extension as Active" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: The observability extension on a Slurm cluster’s Extensions tab, with Actions → Edit&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The edit page sets a collection level for each metric category.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-6-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-6-1.png" alt="Edit HyperPod observability page for a Slurm cluster showing metric categories with collection levels, host profiling, and metric store" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Editing observability on a Slurm cluster: metric categories, host profiling, and the metric store&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To install the extension with the AWS CLI instead:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker create-cluster-extension \
    --cluster-arn &amp;lt;cluster-arn&amp;gt; \
    --extension-name sagemaker-observability \
    --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt; \
    --configuration-values '{
        "metricsProvider": {
            "clusterMetrics": {"level": "ENABLED", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30},
            "nodeMetrics": {"level": "BASIC", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30},
            "acceleratedComputeMetrics": {"level": "BASIC", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30},
            "jobMetrics": {"level": "ENABLED", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30}
        },
        "profilesProvider": {"hostProfiling": {"enabled": false}}
    }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To turn on EFA metrics, update the extension:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker update-cluster-extension \
    --cluster-arn &amp;lt;cluster-arn&amp;gt; \
    --extension-arn &amp;lt;extension-arn&amp;gt; \
    --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt; \
    --configuration-values '{
        "metricsProvider": {
            "clusterMetrics": {"level": "ENABLED", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30},
            "nodeMetrics": {"level": "ADVANCED", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30},
            "acceleratedComputeMetrics": {"level": "ADVANCED", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30},
            "networkMetrics": {"level": "ENABLED", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30},
            "jobMetrics": {"level": "ENABLED", "collectionIntervalSeconds": 30}
        }
    }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Check the status with &lt;code&gt;aws sagemaker describe-cluster-extension --cluster-arn &amp;lt;cluster-arn&amp;gt; --extension-arn &amp;lt;extension-arn&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-confirm-metrics-are-streaming"&gt;Step 5: Confirm metrics are streaming&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the cluster is ready, navigate to the cluster’s detail page. The new &lt;strong&gt;Observability&lt;/strong&gt; tab displays an overview with:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Nodes:&lt;/strong&gt; total node count (for example, 128).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Accelerators:&lt;/strong&gt; total GPU/accelerator count (for example, 1024).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Jobs:&lt;/strong&gt; active and recent training job count.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Avg. GPU utilization:&lt;/strong&gt; fleet-wide average (for example, 87.3%).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Avg. vCPU utilization:&lt;/strong&gt; fleet-wide average (for example, 76.8%).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Avg. memory utilization:&lt;/strong&gt; fleet-wide average (for example, 83.5%).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following image shows the Cluster Observability tab with overview metrics, active and recent training jobs, and active issues:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-7.png" alt="Cluster Observability tab with overview metrics, active and recent training jobs, and active issues" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: The new Observability tab — fleet overview, active training jobs, and detected issues&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cloudwatch-dashboard"&gt;CloudWatch dashboard&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From the Observability tab, choose &lt;strong&gt;Open dashboard&lt;/strong&gt; to navigate to the CloudWatch SageMaker Insights experience. The dashboard provides several views:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="diagnostic-summary-1"&gt;Diagnostic summary&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Diagnostic summary shows incidents detected across your HyperPod clusters. Pinpoint the root cause whether it is a hardware fault, a network issue, throttling, or overheating.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose any incident card to see more details. Each card names the affected job, cluster, accelerator, and rank.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the Diagnostic summary tab:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-8.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-8.png" alt="Diagnostic summary tab listing detected incidents with severity, job, cluster, accelerator, issue type, and a cross-layer summary" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: Diagnostic summary where each incident card names the job, cluster, accelerator, and issue type, with a cross-layer explanation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows an incident detail:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-9-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-9-1.png" alt="Incident detail showing overview, detection, resolution, affected job and instance, and network evidence charts against the fleet average" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 9: Incident detail showing the affected job, cluster, instance, and accelerator, with evidence charts against the fleet average&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="hardware-tab"&gt;Hardware tab&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Hardware tab gives a device-level view of your accelerator fleet. The fleet topology map shows every device’s state (Active, Idle, and Unhealthy). The device table lists the accelerator type, owning team, utilization, temperature, EFA usage, status, and the workload running on each device. You can search and filter by status, accelerator, or team to find an unhealthy device quickly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the Hardware tab:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-10.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-10.png" alt="Hardware tab with fleet topology of device states and a device details table" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 10: Hardware tab showing fleet topology and per-device accelerator, team, utilization, temperature, EFA, status, and workload&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="workload-tab"&gt;Workload tab&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Workload tab lists active and recent training jobs with their type (for example, distributed training, fine-tuning, or reinforcement learning), status, and duration. It also shows resource usage per job: EFA, GPU and CPU utilization, and memory. The tab flags jobs with abnormal network usage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the Workload tab:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-11.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-11.png" alt="Workload tab listing training jobs with type, status, duration, and per-job EFA, GPU, CPU, and memory usage." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 11: Workload tab showing training jobs with per-job EFA, GPU, CPU, and memory usage&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can select a job to view more details. The following screenshot shows the job detail view:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-12.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-12.png" alt="Job detail view with core compute, memory and bandwidth, clock and thermal metrics, workload charts, and Profile CPU and Inspect NCCL actions" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 12: Job detail showing accelerator metrics&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-network-tab"&gt;The Network tab&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Network tab summarizes the health of the inter-node communication fabric for distributed workloads. Overview metrics include fleet-average EFA utilization, degraded node count, and retransmits. A breakdown by instance type shows the share of healthy and degraded nodes for each.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the Network tab:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-13-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-13-1.png" alt="Network tab with fleet EFA utilization, degraded nodes, retransmits, average NCCL all-reduce latency, and EFA health by instance type." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 13: Network tab showing fabric overview and EFA health by instance type&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="amazon-managed-grafana-dashboard"&gt;Amazon Managed Grafana dashboard&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From the cluster Observability tab or the Add-ons page, choose &lt;strong&gt;Open dashboard in Grafana&lt;/strong&gt; to access prebuilt Amazon Managed Grafana dashboards. These dashboards give you full-stack visibility into HyperPod clusters, with telemetry from CloudWatch or Prometheus visualized across cluster, node, GPU, network, and job dimensions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; If using Grafana for the first time, you must first configure AWS IAM Identity Center and add Grafana users. Documentation links are provided during cluster creation when you select the Grafana metric store option.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows a prebuilt Grafana dashboard:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-14.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/18/ML-22438-14.png" alt="Prebuilt Amazon Managed Grafana dashboard visualizing full-stack HyperPod telemetry." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 14: Prebuilt Grafana dashboard showing GPU performance metrics per node and GPU&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="now-available"&gt;Now available&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enhanced observability in Amazon SageMaker HyperPod is available today in all AWS Regions where SageMaker HyperPod is available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Learn more:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;Check out the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/introducing-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-a-purpose-built-infrastructure-for-distributed-training-at-scale/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
