		<item>
		<title>Amazon SageMaker Inference: 2026 launches in review, part 2</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-inference-2026-launches-in-review-part-2/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aryaman Kothari]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 20 Nov 2026 00:34:07 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">19955f2885c60f352884dbb3fc318cc9afbc5339</guid>

					<description>This second installment reviews 13 more Amazon SageMaker AI inference launches from 2026, spanning configuration recommendations, fitting more models onto each GPU, serving models too large for one instance, cross-Region routing, and post-launch tuning, bringing the year's total to 26.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;From first endpoint to global fleet: 14 more launches for managed endpoints and HyperPod Inference&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In September, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-inference-2026-year-to-date-launches-in-review/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Inference: 2026 year-to-date launches in review &lt;/a&gt;covered 13 launches that shortened the path from a model to a production endpoint. Most of them answered one question: how do I get this model serving quickly and reliably? This post covers the 14 launches that came next, bringing the total for 2026 to 27.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once a model is in production, the questions change. Teams want to know which configuration the model should run on now that traffic is real, how to fit more models onto GPUs they already pay for, what to do when the next model is too large for one machine, why requests wait in one Region while GPUs sit idle in another, and why latency rose when nobody changed anything. This post takes those questions in the order teams usually meet them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For HyperPod Inference, it listed additional routing strategies, broader support for model serving frameworks, and expanded multi-Region networking. The Inference Gateway and the Global Inference Gateway deliver the routing and multi-Region work, and managed NVIDIA Dynamo is a new serving framework. For managed endpoints, the roadmap pointed to deeper integration with the rest of the AWS AI landscape. Inference recommendations now run from Amazon SageMaker AI Studio, the SageMaker Python SDK, and the coding agents engineers already use, and endpoints can now route requests across Regions and accounts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To keep each section concrete, we follow one hypothetical team through the blog post. In the spring, they launched a customer support assistant on a 70B-parameter model behind a single SageMaker AI endpoint, using the launches from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-inference-2026-year-to-date-launches-in-review/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;part 1&lt;/a&gt;. By the fall, they need to support more product lines, adopt a larger reasoning model, serve customers in a second Region, and keep their GPU bill flat.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-launches-at-a-glance"&gt;The launches at a glance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_145910" style="width: 1754px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-145910" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-145910" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/Screenshot-2026-11-20-at-3.27.51%E2%80%AFPM.png" alt="" width="1744" height="948"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-145910" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The five stages in this post and the launches in each&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choose-the-right-configuration-from-wherever-you-work"&gt;Choose the right configuration from wherever you work&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team picked its first configuration from a benchmark run in April. Since then, they have added four LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) adapters, one for each product line, and the product team has set a target of 500 milliseconds for time to first token (TTFT) at peak load. The April configuration was never tested against either change. The engineers who will re-run the analysis work in notebooks and coding agents, and few of them use the console.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inference recommendations launched in April as an API. Four updates since then make it available in more of the places teams work, and let it answer two harder questions: how many adapters an endpoint can carry, and how much concurrency it can take before it misses a latency target. A new agent skill brings the same expertise into coding agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="inference-recommendations-in-sagemaker-ai-studio-july-2026"&gt;Inference recommendations in SageMaker AI Studio (July 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/launching-ui-for-generative-ai-inference-recommendations-in-amazon-sagemaker-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Launching UI for generative AI inference recommendations in Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The April launch automated instance, container, and optimization selection through APIs. Inference recommendations are now also available in SageMaker AI Studio as a guided, low-code workflow. In Studio, choose &lt;strong&gt;Jobs&lt;/strong&gt;, then &lt;strong&gt;Inference optimization&lt;/strong&gt;. Choose a use-case profile (Interact, Generate, Summarize, or Custom), an optimization goal (minimize latency, maximize throughput, or minimize cost), and a model from SageMaker JumpStart, Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), the Model Registry, or an existing SageMaker model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI benchmarks candidate configurations on real GPU infrastructure using NVIDIA AIPerf and applies techniques that match the goal, such as speculative decoding for throughput or kernel tuning for latency. You compare the ranked results by TTFT, inter-token latency, throughput, and cost, and then deploy the chosen configuration to a real-time endpoint from Studio.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="inference-recommendations-in-the-sagemaker-python-sdk-v3-august-2026"&gt;Inference recommendations in the SageMaker Python SDK v3 (August 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/llm-optimization-integration-for-amazon-sagemaker-python-sdk/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LLM optimization integration for Amazon SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Benchmarking and recommendations previously required SageMaker AI Studio or direct Boto3 calls. They are now part of the SageMaker Python SDK v3 (version 3.17.0 and later), the same interface teams use for training and deployment, so the workflow fits into notebooks, CI pipelines, and MLOps workflows. You can benchmark a live endpoint against your workload traffic, generate ranked recommendations for a latency, throughput, or cost goal, and deploy the top-ranked configuration to a real-time endpoint in a single call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The SDK also supports longer workflows. You can run recommendation jobs in parallel to compare serving frameworks such as LMI and vLLM, and you can resume from a completed job so that deployment happens in a different notebook or pipeline stage without re-running the benchmarks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="lora-adapter-support-in-inference-recommendations-september-2026"&gt;LoRA adapter support in inference recommendations (September 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Developer guide&lt;/em&gt;: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/generative-ai-inference-recommendations-adapters.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Get recommendations for models with LoRA adapters&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many teams use LoRA adapters to specialize one foundation model for several use cases. Each adapter kept in GPU memory reduces the number of concurrent requests the endpoint can serve, and the right balance depends on the instance type, the number of adapters, and the traffic profile.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inference recommendations now accept adapters as Model Package Amazon Resource Names (ARNs), for adapters produced by SageMaker fine-tuning, or as S3 URIs, for adapters from external pipelines such as Hugging Face PEFT. SageMaker AI benchmarks combinations of adapter count and concurrency on real GPU infrastructure and returns ranked results for your workload. The output lists both formats for every adapter. The recommended deployment uses SageMaker Inference Components, with one component for the base model and one for each adapter, so you can add, update, or remove an adapter without disrupting the base model or the other adapters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="sla-based-concurrency-search-september-2026"&gt;SLA-based concurrency search (September 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/right-size-generative-ai-endpoints-with-concurrency-sweeps-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Right-size generative AI endpoints with concurrency sweeps on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Right-sizing an endpoint means finding the highest concurrency it can sustain without breaching latency or error targets. Until now, that meant picking concurrency levels by hand, running a sweep, checking the results against the service level agreement (SLA), and repeating with a narrower range whenever the model or instance changed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inference recommendations now include a search that finds that boundary directly. Set &lt;code&gt;search_recipe&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;max-concurrency-under-sla&lt;/code&gt; and supply targets for any combination of maximum TTFT, maximum time per output token, maximum end-to-end latency, and maximum error rate. The benchmarking engine (AIPerf) starts from a broad range, narrows it based on which probes pass or fail, and converges in fewer iterations than a linear sweep. The result reports the largest passing concurrency (&lt;code&gt;feasible_max&lt;/code&gt;) or, if no level qualifies, the smallest failing one (&lt;code&gt;infeasible_min&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The search uses the same &lt;code&gt;CreateAIWorkloadConfig&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;CreateAIBenchmarkJob&lt;/code&gt; APIs as concurrency sweeps. Optional parameters (&lt;code&gt;concurrency_min&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;concurrency_max&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;search_max_iterations&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;search_initial_points&lt;/code&gt;) bound the cost and speed up convergence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agent-skill-for-optimized-generative-ai-inference-october-2026"&gt;Agent skill for optimized generative AI inference (October 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/new-agent-skill-amazon-sagemaker-optimized-generative-ai-inference-for-your-coding-agent/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;New agent skill: Amazon SageMaker optimized generative AI inference for your coding agent&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Engineers increasingly ask their coding agent which instance and container to use. Without grounded knowledge and access to real benchmarks, the agent can only guess, and a wrong instance choice shows up later as missed latency targets or wasted spend.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The aws-ai-ml skill, available through the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/products/developer-tools/agent-toolkit-for-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Toolkit for AWS&lt;/a&gt;, gives coding agents such as Kiro built-in SageMaker AI inference expertise. Engineers describe what they need in plain language, and the agent can do the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Find the right instance.&lt;/strong&gt; Ranked deployment options with measured throughput, latency, and TTFT for a model in Amazon S3, SageMaker JumpStart, or the Hugging Face Hub.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Benchmark a live endpoint.&lt;/strong&gt; A report from real load covering throughput, P50 and P99 latency, inter-token latency, and supported concurrency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compare runs.&lt;/strong&gt; The percentage change on every metric, which shows whether a configuration change helped.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Answers are grounded in measured performance and delivered as SageMaker Python SDK v3 code that engineers review and run under their own credentials. Setup takes about 10 minutes. The skill works with any agent that supports the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and in SageMaker AI Studio JupyterLab spaces.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the support team, one recommendation job that includes all four adapters, followed by a concurrency search against the 500 millisecond TTFT target, replaces the manual sweeps they ran in April. The engineer who owns it can start the job from a notebook or ask a coding agent to run it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="fit-more-models-onto-each-gpu"&gt;Fit more models onto each GPU&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The support assistant does not run alone. An embedding model, a reranker, and a small classifier that routes tickets live in the same account, and each uses a fraction of a GPU. On a standard endpoint, each model copy still held a whole accelerator, so the team ran out of placement slots while GPU memory sat unused.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="inference-components-in-the-sagemaker-ai-console-october-2026"&gt;Inference Components in the SageMaker AI console (October 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Developer guide:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/realtime-endpoints-deploy-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Inference Components in the SageMaker AI Console&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Inference Components, you can host several models on one endpoint and size and scale each model separately. Until now, they were available only through the SDK and the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI). Every create, resize, and scaling policy had to be scripted, and there was no single view of what each model was using.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the SageMaker AI console, you can now create, view, update, and delete inference components:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create and edit.&lt;/strong&gt; Create an endpoint with inference components, add or delete components later, and change each component’s accelerators, CPU, memory, and copy count.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scale each model.&lt;/strong&gt; Set an automatic scaling policy for each component, down to zero copies, so an idle model stops holding accelerators.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Monitor.&lt;/strong&gt; See alarms and per-component metrics on the same page.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The console calls the same SageMaker AI APIs as the SDK and CLI, so teams can start on the console and automate later, or manage scripted components visually.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="gpu-partitioning-with-nvidia-multi-instance-gpu-october-2026"&gt;GPU partitioning with NVIDIA Multi-Instance GPU (October 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/sagemaker-inference-now-supports-multi-instance-gpu-to-maximize-gpu-utilization-for-model-endpoints/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Inference now supports Multi-Instance GPU to maximize GPU utilization for model endpoints&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/inference-components-accelerator-partitioning.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GPU partitioning&lt;/a&gt; uses NVIDIA Multi-Instance GPU (MIG) technology to divide one physical GPU into as many as seven partitions, depending on the accelerator. Each partition has dedicated GPU compute and memory, with complete hardware isolation. You define the partition layout when you create an endpoint for inference components and choose a partition type for each component. Applications keep invoking models by inference component name, so client code does not change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MIG isolates GPU compute and memory between partitions. Models on the same instance still share host CPU, host memory, networking, storage, and endpoint routing. You pay the standard rate for the full instance-hour, so the saving comes from placing more models on each instance, not from a lower hourly price.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-2.png" alt="Three small models on three separate GPUs compared with the same models sharing partitions of one GPU" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Three small models on three GPUs, compared with the same models in partitions of one GPU&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the support team, the embedding model, the reranker, and the classifier now run in separate partitions of one GPU on a single endpoint, and the team manages every component, including the assistant and its adapters, from the console.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="serve-models-larger-than-one-instance"&gt;Serve models larger than one instance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team’s next model is a reasoning model whose weights alone exceed the GPU memory of any single instance. They have also noticed that long, retrieval-heavy prompts slow token generation for every other user on the endpoint. For a model this size, they move the workload to HyperPod Inference, which gives them dedicated clusters and Kubernetes-native control. The support assistant stays on managed endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="managed-nvidia-dynamo-on-hyperpod-inference-november-2026"&gt;Managed NVIDIA Dynamo on HyperPod Inference (November 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/managed-nvidia-dynamo-on-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managed NVIDIA Dynamo on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Part 1 covered two building blocks for large models on HyperPod: disaggregated prefill and decode in Inference Operator v3.2, and KV-aware routing as part of the managed tiered KV cache. This launch adds multi-node serving and changes who operates the stack. NVIDIA Dynamo is an open source inference framework that provides multi-node sharded serving, disaggregated prefill and decode, and KV-aware routing. Teams could already run Dynamo on their own Kubernetes clusters, but they had to build and operate everything around it: placing all the nodes of a replica together, wiring Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) networking so prefill can hand its KV cache to decode at hardware speed, maintaining serving images, and recovering when one failed node takes down a multi-node replica.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this launch, Dynamo runs as a managed serving engine on HyperPod. Dynamo makes the serving decisions: how the model is sharded, how prefill and decode are split, and where each request goes. HyperPod provides what those decisions depend on:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Capacity and networking.&lt;/strong&gt; GPU capacity and EFA networking, provisioned and wired automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Node recovery.&lt;/strong&gt; Node health monitoring and replacement, with multi-node workers rescheduled automatically when a node fails.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Endpoint operations.&lt;/strong&gt; Autoscaling, endpoint registration, TLS, and observability.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One install and upgrade path.&lt;/strong&gt; The Inference Operator add-on installs Dynamo, the serving images, and the Grove and KAI schedulers as one unit and upgrades them together.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The capabilities Dynamo brings to HyperPod are:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-node (sharded) serving.&lt;/strong&gt; One copy of the model spans several nodes, so you can serve models that do not fit on any single instance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Disaggregated prefill and decode.&lt;/strong&gt; Prompt processing and token generation run on separate GPU pools, each on an instance type suited to its work, so a long prompt no longer slows responses for other users.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;KV-aware routing.&lt;/strong&gt; Each request goes to the worker that already holds its KV cache, which reduces TTFT.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are three ways to start. Teams running Dynamo on self-managed Kubernetes can bring their existing &lt;code&gt;DynamoGraphDeployment&lt;/code&gt; (DGD) manifests largely unchanged. Teams already on HyperPod Inference can update the add-on and add a short &lt;code&gt;dynamoSpec&lt;/code&gt; block to an existing &lt;code&gt;InferenceEndpointConfig&lt;/code&gt;, which HyperPod converts into a DGD, while existing endpoints keep serving. Teams new to both can create a cluster with the add-on and Dynamo enabled from the console. In each case, clients call a standard OpenAI-compatible endpoint, so client code does not change.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Managed Dynamo supports vLLM, SGLang, and TensorRT-LLM, and is available in the Regions where &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod.html#sagemaker-hyperpod-available-regions" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/a&gt; is available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-3.png" alt="Architecture of managed NVIDIA Dynamo running on Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Managed NVIDIA Dynamo on HyperPod Inference&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the support team, the reasoning model runs as one sharded replica across several nodes, with separate prefill and decode pools. When a node fails, HyperPod replaces it and reschedules the replica without a page to the on-call engineer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="send-each-request-to-a-gpu-that-can-serve-it"&gt;Send each request to a GPU that can serve it&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The support assistant now serves customers in North America and Europe, so the team runs endpoints and HyperPod clusters in two Regions. During the US morning peak, requests queue in the US Region while GPUs the team already pays for sit idle in Europe. Some European traffic must also stay in Europe.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Routing decides whether capacity a team owns does useful work. It happens at four levels, and the launches below cover one level each: which instance serves a request inside an endpoint, which pod inside a HyperPod cluster, which cluster across a fleet, and which Regional endpoint across Regions and accounts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="mceTemp"&gt; 
 &lt;div id="attachment_145912" style="width: 1790px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
  &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-145912" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-145912" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/Screenshot-2026-11-20-at-3.29.11%E2%80%AFPM.png" alt="" width="1780" height="942"&gt;
  &lt;p id="caption-attachment-145912" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Routing across Regions and inside each endpoint or cluster, on both deployment paths&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;h3 id="kv-cache-aware-routing-for-endpoints-november-2026"&gt;KV-cache-aware routing for endpoints (November 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-ai-real-time-endpoints-now-support-kv-cache-aware-routing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI real-time endpoints now support KV cache aware routing&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Applications with long prompts or multi-turn conversations repeat a large prefix across requests. A model server can reuse the matching KV cache, but only if the next request reaches the instance that holds it. Prefix-aware routing, covered in part 1, fingerprints the start of each prompt and sends matching prompts to the same instance. Because it works from request history, its view of each instance’s cache becomes less accurate as the model server adds and evicts entries.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;KV-cache-aware routing uses the cache state the model container reports. The container publishes cache creation and eviction events to a central cache index, and SageMaker AI routes each request to an eligible instance that holds the longest matching cached prefix. This raises cache reuse for long-context prompts, multi-turn conversations, and workloads that share dynamic prompt prefixes.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;It works with single-model endpoints and endpoints that use inference components, including LoRA adapters. It supports &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpoint&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;InvokeEndpointWithResponseStream&lt;/code&gt;, and the OpenAI-compatible API for Completions and Chat Completions payloads. You turn it on in the endpoint configuration. The first release requires an AWS-verified model container that implements the cache-event, tokenization, metrics, and OpenAI-compatible interfaces. You can track KV cache hit rate in detailed inference observability.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Which one to use.&lt;/strong&gt; Prefix-aware routing works with any container and needs no container changes. KV-cache-aware routing needs a verified container and tracks the actual cache, which helps most when prefixes vary or caches turn over quickly.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;h3 id="inference-gateway-for-hyperpod-september-2026"&gt;Inference Gateway for HyperPod (September 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introducing Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Inside a HyperPod cluster, capacity and demand rarely line up. One pod can be partway through a long-context request with its KV cache nearly full, while the next pod sits idle or already holds the prompt prefix or LoRA adapter the next request needs. Without visibility into that GPU state, new requests queue behind busy pods, and teams add GPUs to protect latency instead of using the ones they have. Each model also had its own endpoint, so clients had to track one endpoint per model.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;The Inference Gateway is a GPU-aware router that installs with the HyperPod Inference Operator add-on and gives each cluster one OpenAI-compatible endpoint for all of its models. For each request, it reads live signals from every model pod and picks the best one, with weights you can tune per workload:&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;ul&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;KV cache utilization.&lt;/strong&gt; Avoids pods whose cache is nearly full.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Queue depth and running requests.&lt;/strong&gt; Avoids pods with a backlog and balances active work.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prefix cache hit rate.&lt;/strong&gt; Prefers pods likely to hold the prompt prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;LoRA adapter residency.&lt;/strong&gt; Prefers pods that already hold the requested adapter, which avoids swap latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;/ul&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Compared with round-robin routing, it reduced P99 TTFT by up to 97% on mixed GPU generations and by up to 98% under bursty traffic. It is available in all Regions where SageMaker HyperPod is available.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;h3&gt;Global Inference Gateway for HyperPod (November 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-global-inference-gateway-one-endpoint-across-regions-and-clusters/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introducing Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Global Inference Gateway&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;The Inference Gateway routes within a cluster, and each cluster can serve only the requests sent to it. A request can wait on a saturated cluster in one Region while GPUs sit idle in another. Clients had to choose a cluster themselves, and when a cluster failed, an engineer had to move traffic by hand.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;The Global Inference Gateway gives teams one SageMaker-managed endpoint across every cluster they register. For each request, it scores clusters on queue depth, free KV cache, proximity to the caller, and recent error rate, and sends the request to the best one over the AWS private backbone. The Inference Gateway in that cluster still picks the pod. Requests authenticate with SigV4 and IAM. When a cluster becomes saturated or unhealthy, traffic shifts to the next-best cluster in under 30 seconds with no action from the client, and the cluster rejoins the pool when it recovers. Routing weights are tunable, with defaults if you leave them unset.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Adding or removing a model or cluster does not change anything for the applications that call the endpoint. No cluster is reachable until you publish it, and the published resources stay in your account. The Global Inference Gateway is available in the Regions listed in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-model-deployment-global-inference-gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS documentation.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-145938" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/20/Screenshot-2026-11-20-at-3.36.16%E2%80%AFPM.png" alt="" width="1844" height="886"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;h3 id="cross-region-inference-routing-in-amazon-sagemaker-ai"&gt;Cross-Region Inference Routing in Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/h3&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Blog:&lt;/em&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/keep-inference-running-through-regional-capacity-shortfalls-with-cross-region-inference-routing-in-amazon-sagemaker-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Keep inference running through regional capacity shortfalls with cross-Region inference routing in Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;On managed endpoints, the same idea takes the form of the Cross-Region Inference Routing, managed routing that sends real-time and asynchronous requests across Regional endpoints a team has already deployed, in the same account or in different accounts. You group endpoints that serve the same model under a routing target, and the application invokes the target instead of a specific Regional endpoint. The router does not add capacity, replicate models, or deploy endpoints, and the existing invocation path keeps working.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;The router replaces the failover logic teams build themselves today:&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;ul&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One invocation path.&lt;/strong&gt; SageMaker tries the local endpoint first and moves traffic to another registered endpoint when the local one cannot serve a request.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One routing policy.&lt;/strong&gt; The policy combines endpoint metrics with service-side capacity signals, including insufficient capacity errors that customers cannot observe on their own.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private connectivity.&lt;/strong&gt; Cross-Region traffic runs over AWS PrivateLink with a managed cross-account authorization model, so routing to another Region or account does not need internet or NAT gateways.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Overflow and residency guardrails.&lt;/strong&gt; The endpoint owner can cap how much cross-Region traffic a fallback endpoint accepts, and the application can restrict which Regions may serve a request.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;/ul&gt; 
 &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
  &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-5.png" alt="Cross-Region Inference Router request flow between Regional endpoints" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Cross-Region Inference Router request flow&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;The two cross-Region launches apply the same idea on each deployment path. The team keeps ownership of its clusters and endpoints, and SageMaker supplies the routing, the private connectivity, and the failover.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;table border="1px" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
  &lt;tbody&gt; 
   &lt;tr&gt; 
    &lt;td width="134"&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="230"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Global Inference Gateway&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="235"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cross-Region Inference Router&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;/tr&gt; 
   &lt;tr&gt; 
    &lt;td width="134"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deployment path&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="230"&gt;HyperPod Inference&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="235"&gt;Managed endpoints&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;/tr&gt; 
   &lt;tr&gt; 
    &lt;td width="134"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Routes across&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="230"&gt;Registered HyperPod clusters in any Region&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="235"&gt;Registered Regional endpoints, across Regions and accounts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;/tr&gt; 
   &lt;tr&gt; 
    &lt;td width="134"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Connectivity&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="230"&gt;AWS private backbone, SigV4 and IAM&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="235"&gt;AWS PrivateLink, managed cross-account authorization&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;/tr&gt; 
   &lt;tr&gt; 
    &lt;td width="134"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Failover&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="230"&gt;Automatic, in under 30 seconds&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="235"&gt;Automatic, when the local endpoint cannot serve&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;/tr&gt; 
   &lt;tr&gt; 
    &lt;td width="134"&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Owner controls&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="230"&gt;A cluster is reachable only after you publish it&lt;/td&gt; 
    &lt;td width="235"&gt;Overflow caps per fallback endpoint and Region restrictions per application&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;/tbody&gt; 
 &lt;/table&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;For the support team, the US morning peak now overflows to capacity in Europe on both paths, and requests that must stay in Europe are restricted to European Regions in the router policy.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;h2 id="keep-endpoints-tuned-after-launch"&gt;Keep endpoints tuned after launch&lt;/h2&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Two months after the move, the team adds retrieval to the support assistant, and the average prompt length roughly doubles. TTFT is now higher than it was at launch, but nothing has failed, so every alarm stays green. When the team finds a better configuration, they will need to roll it out on large GPU instances that are hard to get more of.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;h3 id="continuous-performance-monitoring-november-2026"&gt;Continuous performance monitoring (November 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Blog: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-ai-now-detects-latency-drift-idle-gpus-and-missed-optimizations-on-your-inference-endpoints/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI now detects latency drift, idle GPUs, and missed optimizations on your inference endpoints&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI now continuously monitors your generative AI inference endpoints and tells you when a change could improve performance or lower cost. Monitoring is on by default for SageMaker AI inference endpoints, with no setup required, and you can opt out of endpoints. SageMaker AI looks only at operational signals: latency, GPU utilization, and the endpoint’s serving configuration. It does not read your requests or responses. When a finding clears its confidence threshold, a notification appears on the AWS Management Console, and the same message arrives by email for teams that work in notebooks and the CLI. The recommended action opens the SageMaker AI agentic experience in SageMaker AI Studio with the finding already filled in, so the analysis, the comparison, and the deployment change happen in one conversation. Nothing deploys until you apply it, and you can turn monitoring off at any time.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;There are three types of findings:&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;ul&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Latency drift after a traffic change.&lt;/strong&gt; When a change such as added retrieval lengthens prompts, TTFT rises. The finding quantifies the regression week over week and recommends re-running inference recommendations against recent traffic to find a configuration that brings TTFT back down.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A configuration gap present since deployment.&lt;/strong&gt; The endpoint was never well tuned for its workload, and the finding points to the change that would close the gap.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Idle GPU capacity.&lt;/strong&gt; When capacity stays unused week after week, the finding flags a chance to right-size the endpoint and reduce cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;/ul&gt; 
 &lt;h3 id="in-place-rolling-model-updates-november-2026"&gt;In-place rolling model updates (November 2026)&lt;/h3&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;A standard rolling update replaces an endpoint one batch at a time, and every batch runs on newly provisioned instances. On a large GPU fleet, that means finding new capacity at every step, and an update can fail partway through when the next batch of instances is not available. Each released instance must also finish cleanup before it can be used again, which slows the rollout.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI now updates models in place. It provisions one small batch of additional instances at the start, shifts traffic to that batch, and then updates the instances it frees, reusing them to roll the change across the rest of the fleet. The update still rolls out in stages, but it needs new capacity only once. For a fleet of 20 instances with a batch size of 2, the update acquires 2 new instances instead of 20. Customers do not need to change anything. As with container caching in part 1, the improvement applies automatically to rolling model updates.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
  &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22497-6.png" alt="Standard rolling update acquiring new instances at each step compared with an in-place update that reuses the fleet" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
  &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: A rolling update acquires new instances at every step. An in-place rolling update acquires one batch at the start and reuses the rest of the fleet&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;For the support team, a Drift Watch finding quantifies the TTFT regression and opens a new recommendation job against recent traffic. They roll out the updated model with one batch of extra instances, instead of finding new GPU capacity for every instance in the fleet.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;h2 id="summary-13-launches-continuing-from-part-1"&gt;Summary: 14 launches, continuing from part 1&lt;/h2&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;The numbering continues from the 13 launches in part 1.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;blockquote&gt;
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;14. Inference recommendations in SageMaker AI Studio (July 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; A guided, low-code workflow for ranked, benchmarked configurations, with deployment from Studio.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;15. Inference recommendations in the SageMaker Python SDK v3 (August 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Benchmark, recommend, and deploy from a notebook or pipeline, with parallel and resumable jobs.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;16. LoRA adapter support in inference recommendations (September 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Benchmarks adapter count against concurrency and deploys adapters as inference components.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;17. SLA-based concurrency search (September 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Finds the highest concurrency that meets TTFT, latency, and error-rate targets.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;18. Agent skill for optimized generative AI inference (October 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Instance selection, benchmarking, and run comparison from MCP-compatible coding agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;19. Inference Components in the SageMaker AI console (October 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Create, scale, and monitor each model on a multi-model endpoint without scripts.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;20. GPU partitioning with NVIDIA MIG (October 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Up to seven isolated partitions per GPU, assigned per inference component.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;21. Managed NVIDIA Dynamo on HyperPod Inference (November 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Multi-node and disaggregated serving, with HyperPod operating the platform underneath.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;22. KV-cache-aware routing (November 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Routes each request to the instance that holds the longest cached prefix, based on cache events from the container.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;23. Inference Gateway for HyperPod (September 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; One endpoint per cluster with GPU-aware routing, and up to 97% lower P99 TTFT than round-robin.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;24. Global Inference Gateway for HyperPod (November 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; One endpoint across clusters and Regions, with failover in under 2 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;25. Cross-Region Inference Router.&lt;/strong&gt; Routes across Regional and cross-account endpoints over PrivateLink, with overflow and residency guardrails.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;26. Continuous performance monitoring.&lt;/strong&gt; Detects latency drift, configuration gaps, and idle capacity, with a guided fix in SageMaker AI Studio.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;27. In-place rolling model updates (November 2026).&lt;/strong&gt; Rolls out model updates with one batch of additional instances at the start, then reuses existing instances across the fleet.&lt;/p&gt;
 &lt;/blockquote&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;By the end of the year, the support team serves four product lines from one base model, runs its small models on shared GPUs, serves a reasoning model that no single instance can hold, and routes traffic across two Regions with residency controls. It did not need to build its own benchmarking harness, failover logic, or drift monitoring to get there.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-sagemaker-inference-2026-year-to-date-launches-in-review/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; argued that advantage in AI inference comes from operating an efficient inference stack. These 14 launches apply that argument to the months after launch day. The AWS Experience-Based Acceleration program can help your team put them to work by benchmarking your workloads and configuring your inference stack against your SLAs and cost targets.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;h2 id="what-is-next"&gt;What is next&lt;/h2&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;On managed endpoints, we plan to extend in-place rolling updates in early 2027 with scale-in-first updates, which remove the need for any additional instances during a model update.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Documentation and getting-started guides for every capability in this post are in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/whatis.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;. Start with the section that matches your current problem. If your traffic has changed since you deployed, re-run inference recommendations. If you are short on placement slots, move small models onto partitioned GPUs. If requests queue while capacity you own sits idle, register your clusters and endpoints for cross-Region routing.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Use Kimi Code CLI with models on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/use-kimi-code-cli-with-models-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Alex Thewsey]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 20 Nov 2026 00:33:30 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">71f08435a376a063de0a38fc52648940b0309672</guid>

					<description>Kimi Code CLI v2.7.0 adds a native Amazon Bedrock provider, so you can connect the open source coding agent to models like Kimi K3.1 Pro using your standard AWS credential chain (named profiles or SSO) instead of a separate API key. This post shows how to configure the provider and send your first request.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/moonshotai/kimi-code" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kimi Code CLI&lt;/a&gt; is an open source coding agent from Moonshot AI, the creator of the Kimi series of open-weight foundation models. Previously, using Kimi Code with models on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; meant configuring a generic connection “provider” with an Amazon Bedrock API key. For many AWS customers, this meant introducing an extra credential to obtain and protect.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With v2.7.0, Moonshot AI adds a native bedrock provider to the CLI. You can now configure the agent to use your standard AWS credential chain, including named profiles and single sign-on (SSO), instead of creating and managing a separate API key.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show you how to connect Kimi Code CLI to models on Amazon Bedrock using your existing AWS credentials. We use &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-kimi-k3-1-pro-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kimi K3.1 Pro Highspeed&lt;/a&gt; as an example, which supports streaming, configurable reasoning, image input, and client-side tool calling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-new"&gt;What’s new&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock provides pay-per-token inference for a range of open and proprietary models from leading providers, available through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/apis.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;standard APIs&lt;/a&gt;. Processing stays within the AWS data boundary, as detailed in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/data-protection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data protection&lt;/a&gt; section of the User Guide. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model cards&lt;/a&gt; detail which APIs and features are supported for each model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In older versions of the Kimi Code CLI, you could connect to Amazon Bedrock through a provider with &lt;code&gt;type = "openai"&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;type = "openai_responses"&lt;/code&gt;. These use the OpenAI-compatible &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-chat-completions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Chat Completions&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-responses-api.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Responses&lt;/a&gt; APIs, respectively. This approach required you to set the regional &lt;code&gt;base_url&lt;/code&gt; and provide an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/api-keys.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock API key&lt;/a&gt;. The key could be short-term or long-term, but it was a separate credential for you to obtain and manage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The native provider changes that setup:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use your existing AWS authentication workflow.&lt;/strong&gt; The &lt;code&gt;bedrock&lt;/code&gt; provider can resolve temporary credentials through the standard &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt; credential chain. You can use the default chain or select a named profile, including a profile backed by SSO.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Override the protocol for each model, if needed.&lt;/strong&gt; The provider infers an API format and regional endpoint from the model ID, but you can change them as detailed in the “Override endpoint URL and protocol” section of this post.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS recommends short-term credentials over long-term credentials. This new path reduces reliance on manually managed API keys, but it doesn’t change your responsibility to protect AWS credentials and scope permissions appropriately.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The native provider handles authentication and request routing in four steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resolve AWS credentials.&lt;/strong&gt; Unless you configure an Amazon Bedrock API key, the bedrock provider uses the standard AWS credential chain in your environment. You can set &lt;code&gt;AWS_PROFILE&lt;/code&gt; to select a specific &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-configure-files.html#cli-configure-files-using-profiles" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;named profile&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generate a short-lived bearer token.&lt;/strong&gt; The provider uses &lt;a href="https://www.npmjs.com/package/@aws/bedrock-token-generator" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;span class="citation" data-cites="aws/bedrock-token-generator"&gt;@aws/bedrock-token-generator&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to create a short-term Amazon Bedrock API key, and renews it when necessary. You don’t need to place any AWS access key or manually generated Amazon Bedrock key in the Kimi Code &lt;code&gt;config.toml&lt;/code&gt;. However, an explicit key given through &lt;code&gt;api_key&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;api_key_env&lt;/code&gt; takes precedence if you provide it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Build the regional endpoint.&lt;/strong&gt; The provider resolves &lt;code&gt;AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;AWS_DEFAULT_REGION&lt;/code&gt; and constructs the appropriate &lt;code&gt;bedrock-runtime&lt;/code&gt; endpoint for the model’s inferred API. For Kimi models, this resolves to the recommended OpenAI-compatible Chat Completions path.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Call the selected inference profile.&lt;/strong&gt; The model alias maps to an Amazon Bedrock model or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-profiles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;inference profile&lt;/a&gt; ID. This example uses the global inference profile &lt;code&gt;global.moonshotai.kimi-k3.1&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;code&gt;global.&lt;/code&gt; prefix selects the profile, while &lt;code&gt;AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt; determines the regional endpoint that receives your request.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The CLI then streams the model response and can use the model’s client-side tool-calling support during coding tasks. Authentication, authorization, model access, and network connectivity must all succeed for a request to complete. The ephemeral API keys generated by the provider are valid for up to 12 hours or your AWS session length, whichever is shorter.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For this walkthrough, we use Kimi K3.1 Pro Highspeed as an example model, accessed through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/global-cross-region-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;global cross-Region inference&lt;/a&gt; for broadest availability and lowest cost. Review the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt; to understand expected costs before using the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to the target model and inference profile in a supported source AWS Region. Check the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-moonshot-ai-kimi-k3-1-highspeed.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kimi K3.1 Pro Highspeed model card&lt;/a&gt; for current Region and inference-profile support.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Kimi Code CLI v2.7.0 or later. Follow the &lt;a href="https://github.com/MoonshotAI/kimi-code/blob/main/docs/en/guides/getting-started.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kimi Code CLI installation guide&lt;/a&gt; and run &lt;code&gt;kimi --version&lt;/code&gt; to check your installation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-install.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CLI&lt;/a&gt;, to follow the profile and identity-check commands in this post. The optional &lt;code&gt;aws login&lt;/code&gt; workflow requires AWS CLI v2.32.0 or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; identity (user or assumed role) with permissions to invoke the target model and inference profile in your chosen AWS Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example IAM &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/access_policies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;policy&lt;/a&gt; grants the permissions used by this walkthrough, for the Kimi K3.1 Pro Highspeed model and global inference profile only. Replace &lt;code&gt;${AWS_REGION}&lt;/code&gt; with your source Region (for example, &lt;code&gt;us-west-2&lt;/code&gt; for US West Oregon) and &lt;code&gt;${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}&lt;/code&gt; with your 12-digit AWS account ID:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "BedrockShortTermBearerToken",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "bedrock:CallWithBearerToken",
      "Resource": "*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": {
          "bedrock:bearerTokenType": "SHORT_TERM"
        }
      }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "BedrockReadKimiK31InferenceProfile",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "bedrock:GetInferenceProfile",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock:${AWS_REGION}:${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:inference-profile/global.moonshotai.kimi-k3.1"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "BedrockInvokeKimiK31GlobalProfile",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "bedrock:InvokeModel",
        "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock:${AWS_REGION}:${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:inference-profile/global.moonshotai.kimi-k3.1"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "BedrockInvokeKimiK31ModelsThroughGlobalProfile",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "bedrock:InvokeModel",
        "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
      ],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock:${AWS_REGION}::foundation-model/moonshotai.kimi-k3.1",
        "arn:aws:bedrock:::foundation-model/moonshotai.kimi-k3.1"
      ],
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": {
          "bedrock:InferenceProfileArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:${AWS_REGION}:${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:inference-profile/global.moonshotai.kimi-k3.1"
        }
      }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "BedrockInvokeKimiK31DefaultProject",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "bedrock:InvokeModel"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock:${AWS_REGION}:${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:project/default",
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": {
          "bedrock:ModelArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:${AWS_REGION}:${AWS_ACCOUNT_ID}:inference-profile/global.moonshotai.kimi-k3.1"
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The foundation-model ARN without a Region or account ID is intentional and required for global cross-Region routing. The &lt;code&gt;BedrockInvokeKimiK31DefaultProject&lt;/code&gt; statement is only required if using the &lt;code&gt;openai_responses&lt;/code&gt; protocol, not with the default &lt;code&gt;openai&lt;/code&gt; (Chat Completions).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Security of your AWS environment is a &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/shared-responsibility-model/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;shared responsibility&lt;/a&gt;. Review and grant IAM permissions according to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/getting-started-reduce-permissions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;principle of least privilege&lt;/a&gt;, particularly for production environments. Consider using a dedicated IAM &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;role&lt;/a&gt; for Kimi Code CLI, separate from your regular AWS activities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started-with-the-bedrock-provider"&gt;Get started with the &lt;code&gt;bedrock&lt;/code&gt; provider&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After your prerequisites are ready, follow these steps to configure AWS authentication, add the provider and model, and send a test request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-configure-aws-credentials"&gt;Step 1: Configure AWS credentials&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-chap-authentication.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;different ways to authenticate with the AWS CLI&lt;/a&gt;, depending on your organization’s setup. With &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-configure-files.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Named profiles&lt;/a&gt;, you can configure multiple AWS contexts and switch between them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If you use SSO through &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/identity-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS IAM Identity Center&lt;/a&gt;, run &lt;code&gt;aws configure sso --profile YOUR_PROFILE_NAME&lt;/code&gt; to set up a profile, then run &lt;code&gt;aws sso login --profile YOUR_PROFILE_NAME&lt;/code&gt; to authenticate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;To use AWS Management Console credentials with AWS CLI v2.32.0 or later, run &lt;code&gt;aws login --profile YOUR_PROFILE_NAME&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/cli-configure-sign-in.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;console-credential login guide&lt;/a&gt; also describes a &lt;code&gt;credential_process&lt;/code&gt; configuration for tools that don’t support the cached login session directly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Omit the &lt;code&gt;--profile&lt;/code&gt; arguments to use your default AWS profile instead of a named one.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whichever approach you choose, your session resolves to an IAM identity (user or role) that needs the Amazon Bedrock permissions from the prerequisites. A Region configured in an AWS CLI profile applies to AWS CLI commands, but you must still set the Region explicitly for Kimi Code in Step 2.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Verify that the AWS CLI resolves your intended identity. Omit &lt;code&gt;--profile YOUR_PROFILE_NAME&lt;/code&gt; if you use the default profile:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sts get-caller-identity --profile YOUR_PROFILE_NAME&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-add-the-provider-and-model"&gt;Step 2: Add the provider and model&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open or create &lt;code&gt;~/.kimi-code/config.toml&lt;/code&gt; in a text editor. If you set &lt;code&gt;KIMI_CODE_HOME&lt;/code&gt;, use &lt;code&gt;$KIMI_CODE_HOME/config.toml&lt;/code&gt; instead. If you have an existing file, back it up and merge these entries with your current settings rather than overwriting unrelated providers or models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;YOUR_SOURCE_REGION&lt;/code&gt; with your supported source Region (for example, &lt;code&gt;us-west-2&lt;/code&gt;). To use a named profile, replace &lt;code&gt;YOUR_PROFILE_NAME&lt;/code&gt; and uncomment the &lt;code&gt;AWS_PROFILE&lt;/code&gt; line: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;default_model = "bedrock-kimi-k3-1"

[providers.bedrock]
type = "bedrock"

[providers.bedrock.env]
AWS_REGION = "YOUR_SOURCE_REGION"
# AWS_PROFILE = "YOUR_PROFILE_NAME"

[models.bedrock-kimi-k3-1]
provider = "bedrock"
model = "global.moonshotai.kimi-k3.1"
max_context_size = 1000000
capabilities = ["always_thinking", "thinking", "image_in", "tool_use"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;The model alias &lt;code&gt;bedrock-kimi-k3-1&lt;/code&gt; is a local name used by Kimi Code CLI. The model value is the Amazon Bedrock global inference-profile ID. The example declares a 1-million-token context window and explicit reasoning, image-input, and tool-use capabilities. It omits &lt;code&gt;api_key&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;api_key_env&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK&lt;/code&gt;, so the provider uses ambient AWS credentials. A configured Amazon Bedrock key would take precedence.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Save the file. The &lt;code&gt;[providers.bedrock.env]&lt;/code&gt; table supplies configuration to the provider. It doesn’t export variables into your shell.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-send-a-request-and-start-a-coding-session"&gt;Step 3: Send a request and start a coding session&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run a small non-interactive coding request: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kimi --model bedrock-kimi-k3-1 --prompt "Write a Python function that checks whether a string is a palindrome. Include a short usage example."&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm that the CLI streams a response without an authentication, authorization, Region, model-access, or network error. This request provides a more meaningful connectivity check than a provider status indicator.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Start an interactive session in a project directory: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cd /path/to/your/project
kimi&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Because you set &lt;code&gt;default_model&lt;/code&gt;, the session selects &lt;code&gt;bedrock-kimi-k3-1&lt;/code&gt; automatically. Ask the agent to explain the repository or propose a small change. Review its proposed file modifications and shell commands before approving them.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configuration-best-practices"&gt;Configuration best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the time of writing, the native bedrock provider has several configuration considerations. These practices help you avoid ambiguous status information and mismatched settings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="use-the-configuration-file-to-set-up-the-provider"&gt;Use the configuration file to set up the provider&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the time of writing, Kimi Code’s desktop and web provider-creation interfaces don’t expose all the provider environment fields used by this credential-chain setup. Edit &lt;code&gt;config.toml&lt;/code&gt; directly to set up the Amazon Bedrock provider. For further details, refer to &lt;a href="https://www.kimi.com/code/docs/en/kimi-code-cli/configuration/providers.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model providers&lt;/a&gt; in the Kimi Code documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="declare-model-capabilities-explicitly"&gt;Declare model capabilities explicitly&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kimi Code doesn’t auto-detect the capabilities of most models on Amazon Bedrock, including Kimi models. Check the Amazon Bedrock model card for each target model and explicitly declare its capabilities to let Kimi Code make use of them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-card-moonshot-ai-kimi-k3-1-highspeed.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kimi K3.1 Pro Highspeed&lt;/a&gt; supports &lt;code&gt;always_thinking&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;thinking&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;image_in&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;tool_use&lt;/code&gt;, but not &lt;code&gt;audio_in&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;video_in&lt;/code&gt;, as reflected in the &lt;code&gt;config.toml&lt;/code&gt; example shown earlier.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These declarations describe client behavior. You should only include capabilities that are supported for each target model and Amazon Bedrock API endpoint. Validate tool and image workflows for your application rather than relying on the capability labels alone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="keep-region-settings-consistent-and-prefer-cross-region-inference-where-possible"&gt;Keep Region settings consistent and prefer cross-Region inference where possible&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The bedrock provider requires an explicit &lt;code&gt;AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;AWS_DEFAULT_REGION&lt;/code&gt; in Kimi Code’s &lt;code&gt;config.toml&lt;/code&gt; or the shell environment. It can’t infer a Region configured only in &lt;code&gt;~/.aws/config&lt;/code&gt; or through &lt;code&gt;aws configure&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you use cross-Region inference profiles (with prefixes such as &lt;code&gt;global.&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;us.&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;in.&lt;/code&gt;), the configured Kimi Code Region controls the source Region that receives your requests. For more information, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cross-region-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cross-Region inference&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For workloads without data-residency requirements, start with the global inference profile. This model, like many others, is priced 10% lower for global inference than through geographic profiles. Review &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt; for current rates and geographic alternatives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="override-endpoint-url-and-protocol-where-needed"&gt;Override endpoint URL and protocol where needed&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In some cases, the provider’s auto-detection might mistake the API protocol or target URL you want to use for a particular model. For example, you will need to set a different URL to use Amazon Bedrock through a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/vpc-interface-endpoints.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;VPC endpoint&lt;/a&gt; or the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/endpoints.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Mantle endpoints&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the Kimi Code &lt;code&gt;config.toml&lt;/code&gt;, you can:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set the &lt;code&gt;protocol&lt;/code&gt; field to &lt;code&gt;openai&lt;/code&gt; (Chat Completions), &lt;code&gt;openai_responses&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;anthropic&lt;/code&gt; (Messages) to configure a model to use a particular supported wire protocol. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model cards&lt;/a&gt; for which models support which APIs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set the &lt;code&gt;base_url&lt;/code&gt; field to change the target URL, which must include any attached API path like &lt;code&gt;/openai/v1&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;/anthropic&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the following example, we show a configuration to connect to the same model but using the OpenAI Responses API through an Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/vpc-interface-endpoints.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;VPC endpoint&lt;/a&gt; you’ve set up:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-toml"&gt;[models.bedrock-kimi-k3-1]
provider = "bedrock"
model = "global.moonshotai.kimi-k3.1"
max_context_size = 1000000
capabilities = ["always_thinking", "thinking", "image_in", "tool_use"]
protocol = "openai_responses"
base_url = "https://vpce-EXAMPLE-VPCEID.bedrock-runtime.us-west-2.vpce.amazonaws.com/openai/v1"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="treat-connectivity-status-as-a-configuration-indicator"&gt;Treat connectivity status as a configuration indicator&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the Kimi Code desktop and web apps, the provider list can show &lt;code&gt;connected&lt;/code&gt; for a bedrock provider. That status doesn’t validate the configured AWS credentials, IAM permissions, Region, model access, or network path. Don’t treat it as proof of a successful model request. Use the non-interactive prompt to test the complete path, and diagnose the actual response if it fails.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleanup"&gt;Cleanup&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you created a new AWS IAM identity or assigned new permissions to experiment with Kimi K3.1 Pro on Amazon Bedrock, remember to delete them when you’re finished testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You might also want to revert any local Kimi Code CLI provider configurations or AWS CLI profiles you set up. You can even uninstall the CLIs altogether if they’re no longer required. See the uninstall guides for &lt;a href="https://www.kimi.com/code/docs/en/kimi-code-cli/guides/getting-started.html#upgrade-and-uninstall" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kimi Code&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/uninstall.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CLI&lt;/a&gt; for details. Remember that deleting the local AWS configuration or CLI doesn’t by itself revoke IAM permissions or an active AWS session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock inference for the models we showed is charged on a per-token basis, so there’s no persistent cloud infrastructure deployment to clean up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We showed how Moonshot AI’s native bedrock provider in Kimi Code CLI v2.7.0 connects to Kimi K3.1 Pro Highspeed on Amazon Bedrock through the standard AWS credential chain. You no longer need a separately managed Amazon Bedrock API key.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock gives you access to open and proprietary AI models from leading providers, through standard APIs. Its governance controls and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/compliance-validation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;compliance validation&lt;/a&gt; programs can support even strictly regulated workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you’re already using Kimi Code CLI and want to run inference on AWS, upgrade today and give the new streamlined authentication a try.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more information about Kimi K3.1 Pro on Amazon Bedrock, see our &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-kimi-k3-1-pro-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;launch blog post&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-moonshotai-on-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Moonshot AI on AWS samples repository&lt;/a&gt;. To explore supported APIs and features by model, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model cards&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide. To discuss your use case with the Amazon Bedrock team, &lt;a href="https://pages.awscloud.com/Amazon-Bedrock-Contact-Us.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;connect with us&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
