			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Enable AI agents to pay for AWS Marketplace x402 products with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/enable-ai-agents-to-pay-for-aws-marketplace-x402-products-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-payments/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Madhu Samhitha Vangara]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 23:02:16 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Marketplace]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">1b0652cc95393da48c861946cd586856ffb3f573</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments now supports AWS Marketplace, so agents can discover and buy x402-enabled products, APIs, data, and content and pay in USD on the developer's AWS bill, with no stablecoin wallets or API keys. This post walks through a research agent that finds a tool, pays per request through AWS Billing, and answers with sources.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments became &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-payments-is-now-generally-available-enabling-agents-to-transact-safely-and-autonomously-at-scale/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generally available&lt;/a&gt; in August 2026, agents could autonomously pay for paid APIs, MCP servers, and web content over open protocols like x402 and MPP with payment limits and full observability. Today we extend that autonomy to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-process-payment.html#payments-process-payment-aws-billing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;, so agents can discover and buy x402-enabled software, APIs, data, and content, and pay in USD on the agent developer’s AWS bill, removing the need for stablecoin wallets or API keys.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_145820" style="width: 1934px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-145820" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-145820" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/awsbilling-ecosystem-transparent.png" alt="" width="1924" height="704"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-145820" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Overview of AgentCore payments showing the new AWS Billing integration for purchasing on AWS Marketplace&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Developers can also configure and automate discovery through the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-aws-marketplace.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace connector in AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;. Existing guardrails carry over, developers can set spending limits and approved sellers using AgentCore policy, and the payment session cap is enforced by AgentCore payments, so an agent is unable to overspend. Transactions are auditable in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudTrail&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-cost-explorer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost Explorer&lt;/a&gt;. Because agent purchases run through AWS accounts on both sides, agentic purchasing meets the security and auditability enterprises expect.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The x402 catalog on AWS Marketplace is expanding with a growing roster of trusted, verified sellers. Tavily, a live search provider for agentic commerce, is one of them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Agentic commerce isn’t just about giving agents the ability to pay. It’s about giving enterprises the confidence to let them.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;That conviction is driving Tavily’s collaboration with the AWS team on the design of Agentic Commerce. By bringing secure, x402 powered transactions to AWS Marketplace and connecting agents with trusted, KYC verified providers, AWS is laying the foundation for autonomous commerce at production scale.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This is what enterprise ready agentic commerce looks like.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Rotem Weiss, Founder &amp;amp; CEO, Tavily&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-launching-today"&gt;What’s launching today&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today’s launch adds AWS Billing as a payment option in AgentCore payments. An agent can now pay x402-enabled AWS Marketplace sellers per request, in USD, settling on their AWS bill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When an AWS Marketplace seller responds with 402 Payment Required, its response includes a signed quote for the purchase. With AWS Billing, the agent can forward the seller’s x402 response to AgentCore payments. AgentCore payments checks session budgets, then returns a payment token. The agent retries the request to the seller with the payment token included and receives a successful response. AWS bills the charge to the AWS account where Payment Manager is set up, and the charge appears on that account’s AWS bill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To set up AWS Marketplace purchasing, the developer creates a Payment Manager with an AWS Billing connector. The connector only requires you to set up a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/marketplace/latest/buyerguide/buyer-service-linked-role-agentic-commerce.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;service-linked role&lt;/a&gt; to access AWS Marketplace. Then, for each task, developers can create a payment session with a spending limit and give the agent the session ID.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An agent’s first purchase from a seller creates an agreement for the AWS account with that seller, and AWS Marketplace automatically tracks later purchases against it. Each product will then show up as an AWS Marketplace line item. If your organization uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/features/privatemarketplace" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Private Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;, agents can only buy the products it allows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition, to discover new sellers, we are launching a new &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-aws-marketplace.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace connector in AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;. This optional integration helps agents find products and their endpoints. At launch, AWS Billing supports the x402 &lt;em&gt;exact&lt;/em&gt; scheme, where the seller quotes a fixed price for each request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through a research agent that discovers a tool, pays for a single request through AWS Billing, and answers with sources, with nothing set up in advance. We also provide an accompanying GitHub sample that you can use to evaluate the setup in your own AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sample-walkthrough"&gt;Sample walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Suppose a financial analyst asks the agent what was announced about agentic payments this week. The model wasn’t trained on this week’s news, so the agent looks for a search tool. Through AgentCore Gateway, it searches AWS Marketplace, reads the listing from Tavily, an AWS Marketplace seller, and requests the listing’s x402 endpoints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent calls the endpoint and receives an x402 payload with price details. The agent then forwards the x402 payload to AgentCore payments. AgentCore payments checks it against the remaining session budget and returns a payment token. Once these checks are passed, the agent sends the request again with the token attached, and gets the search results. Its answer includes sources and states what it bought and what it cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this workflow, agents can apply their reasoning to decide which AWS Marketplace tool to use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_145822" style="width: 1064px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-145822" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-145822" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/x402-payment-flow.png" alt="" width="1054" height="345"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-145822" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: End-to-end flow of the sample solution using a Strands agent&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Discover.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent calls three read-only gateway tools in order: search listings, get a listing, and list its fulfillment options. Search listings tool only returns endpoints that accept x402. The last call returns the seller’s endpoint URL and the payment protocols it accepts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. Get a quote.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent calls the endpoint with no payment attached. The seller answers with HTTP 402 and puts a signed quote in the &lt;code&gt;PAYMENT-REQUIRED&lt;/code&gt; header. AWS Marketplace checks the signature against the seller’s registered key, which means a quote with an altered price gets rejected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. Pay.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent passes the quote to the &lt;code&gt;ProcessPayment&lt;/code&gt; API with the payment type &lt;code&gt;AWS_BILLING_X402&lt;/code&gt;. If budget remains in the session, AgentCore payments issues a payment token. If not, the call fails and the developer is not charged.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;4. Redeem.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent sends the request again, this time with the token in the &lt;code&gt;PAYMENT-SIGNATURE&lt;/code&gt; header, and the seller returns the content. Payment tokens are valid for about five minutes, so the agent completes steps 2 through 4 in a single pass.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;5. Settle.&lt;/strong&gt; On the developer’s first purchase from a seller, AWS Marketplace creates the agreement with that seller. After that it meters each charge to their AWS bill. Settlement runs asynchronously and shows up in their AWS bill monthly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To create an AWS Billing connector, developers’ AWS accounts must have the AWS Marketplace service-linked role &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/marketplace/latest/buyerguide/buyer-service-linked-role-agentic-commerce.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWSServiceRoleForMarketplaceAgenticCommerce&lt;/a&gt;. AWS Marketplace uses this role to bill AWS Marketplace purchases that their agents make to their AWS account. Refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-prerequisites.html#payments-prereq-aws-billing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation&lt;/a&gt; for more details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="hands-on"&gt;Hands-on&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/02-use-cases/pay-for-x402-aws-marketplace" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;open-source GitHub sample&lt;/a&gt; in the &lt;code&gt;agentcore-samples&lt;/code&gt; repository runs through a demo of the new functionality. It can help you configure AgentCore payments for AWS Marketplace, connect Tavily, run the agent, and inspect the transaction. You can refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-process-payment.html#payments-process-payment-aws-billing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation&lt;/a&gt; for more details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-1-configure-agentcore-payments"&gt;Step 1: Configure AgentCore payments&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;setup_aws_billing.py&lt;/code&gt; creates a payment manager with an AWS Billing connector, plus the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/marketplace/latest/buyerguide/buyer-service-linked-role-agentic-commerce.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace service-linked role&lt;/a&gt; and three separate AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles. The session role can set budgets but can’t pay. Meanwhile the agent role can pay but can’t create sessions, so the agent has no way to raise its own budget.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python setup_aws_billing.py --region us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-2-connect-tavily"&gt;Step 2: Connect Tavily&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;setup_gateway.py&lt;/code&gt; creates an AgentCore Gateway with the AWS Marketplace connector and gives the agent role the appropriate permissions to use it. The gateway’s role is limited to three read-only AWS Marketplace actions. If you choose to skip this step, you can instead set &lt;code&gt;SELLER_URL&lt;/code&gt; in the environment variables to the seller’s endpoint, and the agent will pay that URL only rather than using AgentCore Gateway discovery.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python setup_gateway.py --region us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-3-run-the-agent"&gt;Step 3: Run the agent&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The script creates a payment session as the session role and then runs the agent as the agent role. By default the session budget is $0.05 and the per-request cap is $0.02.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;python strands_marketplace_agent.py "What was announced about agentic payments this week? Cite sources."&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent searches, looks at listings, gets an endpoint, and pays once. In this test run against a $0.01 seller, it compared two listings before buying:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;Tool calls: search_marketplace -&amp;gt; get_remaining_budget -&amp;gt; search_marketplace -&amp;gt; search_marketplace -&amp;gt; get_listing -&amp;gt; get_listing -&amp;gt; get_seller_endpoints -&amp;gt; get_seller_endpoints -&amp;gt; fetch_paid_api -&amp;gt; get_remaining_budget
Payments this run: 1, total $0.01&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-4-inspect-the-transaction"&gt;Step 4: Inspect the transaction&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the run finishes, the sample will print the payment with its amount, AWS Marketplace product ID, and &lt;code&gt;processPaymentId&lt;/code&gt;. The AWS Marketplace meters charges asynchronously, so the Tavily line item takes a moment to appear on your bill. Agent purchases will also show up in AWS CloudTrail and AWS Cost Explorer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="step-5-clean-up"&gt;Step 5: Clean up&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Run &lt;code&gt;cleanup.py&lt;/code&gt; to delete resources. Make sure to validate deletion to avoid incurring additional resource charges.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-process-payment.html#payments-process-payment-aws-billing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments and AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt; provide the infrastructure for agents that can transact. The research agent in this post goes from “I need live web search” to sourcing an answer on its own, discovering premium paid content through tools, and paying for requests that settle on the developer’s AWS bill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With AgentCore payments, developers can build with open protocols like x402, which opens them to a growing catalog of trusted, verified AWS Marketplace sellers. This gives agents more autonomy and developers more control. Governance is built in, with per-session spending caps and an approved seller list using AgentCore policy, and IAM identities that make it auditable in AWS CloudTrail and AWS Cost Explorer. The agent chooses the right tool for the task, and developers can keep the same billing, budgets, and controls they already trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="resources"&gt;Resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-process-payment.html#payments-process-payment-aws-billing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/02-use-cases/pay-for-x402-aws-marketplace" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub sample&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/marketplace/latest/userguide/agentic-commerce-products.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace seller documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/solutions/ai-agents-and-tools?ams%23interactive-card-vertical%23pattern-data-843993407.filters=((id:GLOBAL%23local-tags-aws-marketplace-agentic-ai-x402-support.and,value:(x402-support)))" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;x402-enabled products in AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/awsmarketplace/aws-marketplace-helps-humans-and-agents-buy-deploy-and-transact/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace launch blog post&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

   &lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/19/ML-22459-5-2.jpg" alt="Chidambaram Muthappan" width="100" height="133"&gt;

  &lt;h3 class="lb-h4"&gt;Chidambaram Muthappan&lt;/h3&gt; 
  &lt;p style="overflow: hidden"&gt;Chidambaram is a Senior Specialist Solutions Architect on the AWS Marketplace team. He helps partners bring their products to customers in AWS Marketplace.&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt;

  &lt;div class="blog-author-image" style="margin-right: 1.0em"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How the BMW Group governs hundreds of agents across thousands of developers</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-the-bmw-group-governs-hundreds-of-agents-across-thousands-of-developers/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dr. Jens Kohl]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 22:20:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Automotive]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">1330a64685170b909926b2b4b2a02424e8e39834</guid>

					<description>When hundreds of agents serve thousands of developers, governance cannot be an afterthought. The BMW Group shares the practices and patterns its R&amp;amp;D teams use to build, govern, and operate AI agents at enterprise scale without sacrificing speed, quality, or compliance.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When hundreds of agents serve thousands of developers, enterprise governance can’t be an afterthought.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the BMW Group’s R&amp;amp;D, more than 12,500 developers work on approximately 500 million lines of vehicle software code. This software is augmented by the BMW Connected Vehicle backend, a cloud-based system of more than 1,300 microservices providing digital services for 24 million vehicles in the field. In regulated industry, the bar for (software) quality, stability, and governance compliance is exceptionally high.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While research, frameworks, and guidelines now facilitate building agents, industrializing and operating high-quality agents in an efficient, scalable manner remains a significant challenge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Scaling agentic AI across a large organization cannot be achieved by isolated teams. It requires collective effort, locally driven experimentation, and active knowledge sharing across the enterprise. This creates a fundamental governance dichotomy: Development teams need the freedom to innovate rapidly, while the enterprise must enforce standards for safety, security, composability, and cost-efficiency. Without that balance, organizations risk a fragmented landscape of AI islands and costly duplicate work. This presents a real challenge given hundreds of globally distributed teams building agents across the BMW Group.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we detail the practices and patterns that support BMW’s R&amp;amp;D teams in building, governing and operating agents at scale without sacrificing speed, quality or compliance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution"&gt;Solution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our premise is straightforward: build agents that can cooperate from the start. This requires an architecture that makes diversity composable, governable, and automatable, without requiring a single framework or toolchain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Central to this architecture is the agent harness, a runtime layer wrapped around a large language model (LLM). It provides the execution loop, tool dispatch, memory management, and observability hooks for autonomous operation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Just as the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/architecture/well-architected/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Well-Architected Framework&lt;/a&gt; standardized cloud infrastructure design, agentic systems need equivalent patterns, reusable blueprints encoding governance, composability, and operational best practices from the start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We organize our solution around five interlocking practices, drawn from hands-on experience building and operating agentic systems at enterprise scale since 2023. Each practice is codified as infrastructure as code (IaC), making adoption repeatable, auditable, and enforceable across teams without blocking DevOps velocity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="govern-proactively-not-retroactively"&gt;Govern proactively, not retroactively&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Governance must be structural from the start and maintained across the full lifecycle. We advocate for three properties of effective agentic governance:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Framework-agnostic and externalized:&lt;/strong&gt; Governance rules such as authentication, authorization, rate limiting and content filtering are enforced at the integration layer. This includes a Model Context Protocol (MCP) gateway, a central agent registry and orchestration boundary, rather than within individual agent codebases. This means governance survives framework migrations, model swaps, and team reorganizations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Domain-sensitive and graduated:&lt;/strong&gt; Not every agent action carries the same risk: drafting documentation, updating customer records, and production database writes demand very different levels of oversight. We implement graduated trust through per-step autonomy levels:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level 0 (Human-in-the-loop):&lt;/strong&gt; Every action requires explicit human approval. This is the starting point for new agents and high-risk operations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level 1 (Human-on-the-loop):&lt;/strong&gt; The agent executes autonomously but surfaces decisions for asynchronous human review. Suitable for medium-risk tasks with defined inputs, outputs, and rollback options.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Level 2 (Autonomous within guardrails):&lt;/strong&gt; The agent operates independently within strictly defined policy boundaries, enforced deterministically rather than through prompt instructions (see below). Reserved for low-risk, well-understood operations with verified track records.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Assign trust levels per step, not per agent. A single workflow can include steps at all three levels, so teams can start conservatively and incrementally increase autonomy as confidence builds, grounded in measurable evaluation data, not assumptions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deterministically enforced:&lt;/strong&gt; Governance runs as deterministic code outside the agent’s reasoning loop. In practice, this happens mainly in the CI/CD pipeline. Permission checks, policy-as-code rules, and compliance validations gate every deployment there, before an agent reaches production. Because these checks run separately from the agent, neither the agent nor its prompts are in a position to override them. Egress controls (recipient allowlisting, outbound secret scanning) help prevent an agent holding confidential data from disclosing it through an otherwise legitimate call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="instrument-verify-and-evaluate-continuously"&gt;Instrument, verify, and evaluate continuously&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Observability in agentic systems goes beyond traditional application monitoring. The real question is no longer whether a service returned an HTTP 200 status, but whether an agent produced a faithful, policy-compliant output, and how this can be demonstrated through observable execution traces. Answering that requires a ground-truth dataset covering common queries and edge cases. This dataset is used for evaluation from day one, so quality is measured against representative and appropriately governed evaluation datasets for evaluation, not synthetic prompts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This infrastructure cannot be retrofitted and must be part of the initial design. The continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipeline enforces this, so agents lacking instrumentation fail validation before evaluation runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We recommend instrumenting three dimensions:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Intent tracing:&lt;/strong&gt; Capture the observable agent trajectory (reasoning steps, tool invocations, and context retrievals) for each execution. OpenTelemetry spans create a queryable lineage from user request to final output.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verification and evaluation as CI:&lt;/strong&gt; Any change to the agent (prompt edits, tool additions, model swaps) triggers the pipeline. &lt;strong&gt;Verification&lt;/strong&gt; runs first with automated regression tests covering agent components and then their interactions. As most harness components are deterministic, these tests are fast and fully automatable. &lt;strong&gt;Evaluation&lt;/strong&gt; runs second, scoring non-deterministic output against the ground-truth dataset, gated on quality thresholds. This sequencing is deliberate: Verification acts as a gate, so evaluations only run on a passing build.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model tiering and routing telemetry:&lt;/strong&gt; When agents route between models, capture the routing decision and its justification, not just the outcome. Tier assignments are derived from evaluation results per task type and poor outcomes flow back as new evaluation cases.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="start-focused-design-for-composition"&gt;Start focused, design for composition&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For distributed cloud systems, the industry solved the complexity of end-to-end workflows by adopting architectural patterns such as microservices or service meshes. The evolution of agentic systems needs to follow a similar trajectory, moving from monolithic, generalist agents toward multiple collaborating specialized agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams should define the problem before considering agents’ functionality. Before writing any orchestration logic, they should produce five concrete artifacts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Clear scope definition:&lt;/strong&gt; what the agent should and shouldn’t do.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Interaction model&lt;/strong&gt;: how users and other agents will invoke it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unambiguous tool definitions:&lt;/strong&gt; each tool and parameter precisely defined.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ground-truth dataset&lt;/strong&gt;: the evaluation reference described above, established before any implementation begins.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluation criteria:&lt;/strong&gt; quality thresholds and benchmarks the agent will be measured against.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This discipline prevents the most common failure modes we observe: agents that take on too many responsibilities accumulate bloated prompts, reducing accuracy and increasing latency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Once a single agent is well-scoped, it must be designed as a composable building block. Therefore, we define a three-tier composition hierarchy:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MCP tools&lt;/strong&gt; are atomic integration contracts. Each tool wraps a single capability (for example, an API call or a database query) without encoding workflow assumptions. This is the stable, reusable layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skills&lt;/strong&gt; are bundled, domain-specific compositions of MCP tools. A code review skill, for example, might chain a static analysis tool, a style-check tool, and an LLM-based reasoning step. Skills encode domain logic but remain portable across workflows.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Flows&lt;/strong&gt; are end-to-end orchestrations of skills, representing complete business processes (for example, an entire PR review pipeline or a requirements-to-deployment cycle).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each tier is validated independently in the CI/CD pipeline. Component tests cover tools and skills, interaction tests cover their composition into flows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These tiers are connected through open standards: MCP and Agent Skills for tool integration, the protocol for agent-to-agent communication, and OpenTelemetry for observability across the resulting interactions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows how the three tiers build on each other, from individual MCP tools at the base to complete flows at the top.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-20698-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-20698-1.png" alt="Figure 1: Three-tier system to build composable agents" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Three-tier system to build composable agents&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="specialize-as-you-learn"&gt;Specialize as you learn&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In production, even well-scoped agents can break down as real-world usage diverges from what was anticipated at design time: query patterns shift, tool-selection logic struggles, and performance degrades unpredictably.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The right response is to decompose complex agents into multiple specialized ones, each with clearer instructions, simpler tool sets, and more focused reasoning. The benefits are concrete:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evolve independently:&lt;/strong&gt; updating a requirements engineering agent does not require touching the coding agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scale individually&lt;/strong&gt;: when scope and interfaces are well-defined, individual agents can be scaled independently without redeploying or resizing the broader system.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Minimized scope:&lt;/strong&gt; a failure or hallucination of one agent does not cascade across the entire workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost savings:&lt;/strong&gt; A narrowly scoped agent can often run on a smaller model, which cuts inference and hosting costs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Complexity can be reduced along two axes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Within an agent:&lt;/strong&gt; decompose capabilities into skills since tools and few-shot examples consume context on every invocation while degrading accuracy and increasing cost. Determine granularity empirically: merge skills the agent rarely selects and split skills that produce different behavior depending on the input.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Across agents&lt;/strong&gt;: delegate work outside an agent’s domain to a specialized peer over the A2A protocol rather than extending the current agent. An agent should grow in capability, not in context.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Critically, since each specialized agent only accesses the data and tools required for its role, this architecture helps to enforce least-privilege by design, a compliance requirement in regulated industries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Realizing these benefits, however, requires that agents can find and interoperate with each other. To prevent the emergence of &lt;em&gt;AI islands&lt;/em&gt;, we maintain a &lt;em&gt;centralized&lt;/em&gt; registry of agents, tools, and skills. This registry serves as:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Discovery mechanism:&lt;/strong&gt; cataloging available agents, tools, and skills based on metadata stored during build.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Governance checkpoint:&lt;/strong&gt; confirming approval of the asset types for production use and maintaining an audit trail.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security checkpoint:&lt;/strong&gt; automated security checks are applied before approval, such as prompt injection and similar issues.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deduplication safeguard:&lt;/strong&gt; identifying equivalent capabilities already maintained by other teams to avoid duplicate work. This check runs automatically at registration, flagging duplicates before approval.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scale-through-codification"&gt;Scale through codification&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The shift from managing individual agents to governing an agent fleet requires agent architectures to be codified. Using Infrastructure-as-code frameworks such as Terraform or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt;, teams provision and manage agent infrastructure with the same rigor applied to any production system:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-grained least-privilege roles:&lt;/strong&gt; scoped per agent or tool.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CI/CD pipelines&lt;/strong&gt;: consistent, validated deployments across accounts and regions, with automated deployment stops on failed tests or metric deterioration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Policy-as-code&lt;/strong&gt;: rules (for example, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/config/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Config&lt;/a&gt;, Open Policy Agent) scanning deployed agents for compliance with organizational standards.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All three are enforced as pipeline gates, validated before registration, not audited after the fact.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The adoption model is deliberately bottom-up with central curation. Individual teams build skills and MCP tools to solve their immediate problems. All teams can contribute by sharing their assets through the registry. A central team builds automatic governance checks as part of the CI pipeline to uphold standards. To support development teams and foster &lt;em&gt;shift-left&lt;/em&gt;, the central team additionally provides building blocks as governance-as-code modules.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This balance avoids both centrally mandated architectures and unmanaged experimentation. As a result, when new operational concerns arise, teams can extend existing patterns rather than redesign their architectures.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="operationalizing-best-practices-across-the-agent-lifecycle"&gt;Operationalizing best practices across the agent lifecycle&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Architectural patterns and governance standards are only effective if they are embedded into the workflows teams use to build and operate agents before and at runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="before-deployment"&gt;Before deployment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Governance begins before a single line of orchestration logic is written. Teams are provided with reference architecture patterns and examples drawn from the registry. These pre-validated blueprints cover common agent configurations, tool integration patterns, and composition structures, which accelerate development while encoding proven defaults. Before anything reaches production, standardized CI/CD controls enforce these practices. Figure 2 shows the four phases of a reference pipeline, which run in order from left to right.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-20698-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-20698-2-1.png" alt="Figure 2: Reference pattern for a four-phase agent deployment pipeline." width="800" height="1296"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Reference pattern for a four-phase agent deployment pipeline.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 1&lt;/strong&gt; from Figure 2 runs fast, deterministic checks: supply chain security (approved registries only), component and interaction testing, evaluation library references, and observability instrumentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 2&lt;/strong&gt; runs security and governance checks in parallel. Static analysis scans the code for issues, and security tests probe the agent in an isolated staging environment. The pipeline also validates egress controls and least-privilege permissions. Two more checks confirm that the agent’s declared autonomy level matches its actual permissions and that its configuration passes the policy-as-code rules. A governance agent accelerates audits by checking the agent against established standards and flagging findings for human review, such as:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scope creep:&lt;/strong&gt; operating beyond originally defined purpose or permissions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prompt bloat:&lt;/strong&gt; unnecessary complexity in instructions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool misuse:&lt;/strong&gt; calling tools outside their intended use case.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Performance degradation:&lt;/strong&gt; declining accuracy, latency, or cost-efficiency.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-143007-2" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-20698/20698-3.mp4?_=2"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 3: Example governance agent workflow&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This assessment surfaces concrete, actionable measures that teams can apply immediately, rather than abstract recommendations. Figure 3 shows the governance agent’s dashboard for an agent maturity assessment. The governance agent provides evidence and actionable recommendations, while accountable humans own governance decisions. Recommendations require human confirmation before being applied, maintaining a &lt;em&gt;four-eyes&lt;/em&gt; principle for accountability. Findings update the agent’s governance status in the registry, while full details are logged separately in a central knowledge base for audit and traceability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 3&lt;/strong&gt; evaluates the agent as a running system: the ground-truth evaluation gate (scores outputs against benchmarks established at design time) and agent performance (latency, token consumption). Figure 4 details the metrics, scoring criteria and the relationships between them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-20698-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/16/ML-20698-4.png" alt="Figure 4: Overview of evaluation metrics" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Overview of evaluation metrics&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 4&lt;/strong&gt; checks for duplicates before registering the agent, its sub-agents, tools, and skills. The following table shows the metadata fields stored per agent in the registry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent metadata&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Core data:&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;unique identifier&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;name&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;description&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;version&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;business owner&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;IT owner&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Capabilities: names, descriptions&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Functions list: name, description, direction: provides/ uses&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Status: plan / active / deprecated&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Link to specification, agent card or documentation&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Hosting mode: on-prem, cloud, cloud-region&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Service level agreement data:&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Target availability in percent&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Target P95 response time in ms&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Support hours / operational support model&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Data governance flags:&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;information classification level&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;personal data processed flag&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;data retention period/ policy&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;request and response payloads logged flag&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;PII data redacted flag&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Human in the loop required flag (required for high-impact / irreversible actions)&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Table 1: Example agent metadata stored in the registry&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If any step fails or metrics deteriorate, the deployment stops with logs for root cause analysis. If all checks pass, the agent is packaged and deployed as a containerized workload, producing consistent, reproducible deployments. This prevents ungoverned agents from reaching production, automates compliance checks, and keeps the registry accurate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="at-runtime"&gt;At runtime&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At runtime, observability has to catch two kinds of drift. Data drift shows up as new or shifting user query patterns, or changes in upstream APIs. Model drift shows as rising error rates or falling tool-selection accuracy. When a customer flags a response, that also triggers an investigation. Alerts for drift, distribution shifts, declining evaluation scores, or direct customer flags prompt a staged response. These events are logged and forwarded to human review. Additionally, teams can run the governance agent continuously to flag drift and operational issues for the responsible team to investigate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="summary"&gt;Summary&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building and operating agents at scale is an engineering discipline, not an experimentation exercise.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Effective governance must be structural from the start, externalized, domain-sensitive, and deterministically enforced. We introduced a three-tier composition hierarchy (MCP tools, skills, and flows) as the architectural foundation for reusable, composable, and interoperable agents, decomposed into specialized agents to contain complexity and scope. We showed how verification and evaluation in every CI/CD run make quality assurance part of everyday development, and how defining agent infrastructure as code lets teams apply the same governance rules to every agent in the fleet. Finally, we operationalized these patterns across the agent lifecycle, from pre-validated reference architectures and structured pipelines to a runtime governance agent that continuously evaluates deployed agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our experience has shown these practices pay off in a few ways. Reusable templates and shared infrastructure reduced development costs. Teams also got agents to production faster, thanks to standard interaction models and automated deployment. Quality improved too, because the pipeline enforces proven patterns at every stage of the lifecycle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Scaling agentic governance is an industry-wide imperative. The shift toward AI and generative-AI-supported workflow automation is already reshaping how engineering organizations operate. The winners will be those who move beyond experimentation and establish the discipline to govern autonomous agent networks at scale. With these practices in place, BMW engineers can focus on solving the problems that matter: Building the ultimate driving machine.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To apply these ideas to your own agents, start with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/architecture/well-architected/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Well-Architected Framework&lt;/a&gt;. This post used it as the model for reusable agent blueprints. To see how BMW codifies governance across its AWS environment, read &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/mt/how-bmw-group-uses-automation-to-achieve-end-to-end-compliance-at-scale-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;How BMW Group uses automation to achieve end-to-end compliance at scale on AWS&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="outlook-and-next-steps"&gt;Outlook and next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The patterns and practices described in this post are a foundation, not a finish line. We are actively extending them across several fronts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Improve architectural guidance:&lt;/strong&gt; publishing best practice guides on architectural trade-offs, composition patterns, and anti-patterns for teams building agents.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expand infrastructure-as-code templates:&lt;/strong&gt; providing agent blueprints as usable IaC modules with built-in security and governance standards by default.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deepen governance compliance testing:&lt;/strong&gt; adding CI/CD test cases verifying governance compliance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automate standards enforcement:&lt;/strong&gt; expanding automated checks and managed compliance-scanning rules across pre-deployment and runtime stages.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extend agentic FinOps:&lt;/strong&gt; Govern the right-sizing of models and tool calls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agentic user guidance:&lt;/strong&gt; integrating described patterns into Archimedes, BMW’s architectural guidance agent, to provide contextual architectural recommendations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Run your whole selling business in AWS Quick</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/run-your-whole-selling-business-in-aws-quick/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Josh Anderson]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 19 Nov 2026 22:16:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Quick]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a212bfea16460bf7a9bea632edeb986e07e0bc27</guid>

					<description>Most sellers do not need another AI product to learn. They need the tools and information they already use brought together, so they can see what is happening in their business and act on it.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Most sellers do not need another AI product to learn. They need the tools and information they already use brought together, so they can see what is happening in their business and act on it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Quick and the Amazon Selling Partner plugin do that together. Quick brings an Amazon seller’s sales data, inventory signals, and marketplace intelligence into one place, where questions become answers and answers become actions. It understands the seller’s full business context, so the seller does not need to re-explain their catalog, their margins, or their competitive landscape in every conversation. It is built on AWS, the same infrastructure Amazon uses to run its own marketplace.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At Amazon Accelerate on September 23, 2026, Amazon announced the Selling Partner plugin and launched it in beta in AWS Quick. This post covers what launched, how to claim the free AWS Quick offer, and what the experience looks like day to day. It also covers how the skills and connectors work when a seller puts them to use.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Limited-time offer: 12 months of AWS Quick Plus, free. Every primary selling account holder worldwide can claim a free 12-month subscription, plus access for 10 additional users you delegate. Sign up by December 31, 2026.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://quick.aws.com/sn?offer_type=sp-quick-promo" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claim your free AWS Quick Plus offer.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;1. Why Quick is right for Amazon sellers&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four things make Quick a fit for how Amazon sellers already work: it learns the business, it reaches beyond the Amazon store, it turns data into decisions, and it protects seller data on infrastructure sellers already trust.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="quick-knows-your-business-not-only-your-prompts"&gt;Quick knows your business, not only your prompts&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick and the Selling Partner plugin connect to a seller’s inventory feeds, sales data, and operational documents. From these, Quick builds a living understanding of the catalog structure, margin thresholds, seasonality, and competitive position. Ask Quick to audit your ASINs for Q4, and it returns a ranked recommendation built on the seller’s real margin data, historical conversion, and inventory availability. Its recommendations get sharper the more the seller works with it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="quick-does-not-stop-at-your-amazon-store"&gt;Quick does not stop at your Amazon store&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A seller’s business runs across many tools: a storefront on another channel, an email inbox, an accounting system, spreadsheets, a supplier portal. Quick connects to where the seller already works and pulls context from across the whole operation. It is a business assistant that happens to know Amazon deeply, so the seller grows overall, not on one channel alone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="it-turns-data-into-decisions-you-can-act-on"&gt;It turns data into decisions you can act on&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sellers are not short on data. They have Brand Analytics, Search Query Performance, advertising reports, inventory health, and listing quality data. The problem is turning those signals into one clear action. Quick does the synthesis. It watches for change, flags what matters, builds the analysis, and presents the decision with the evidence behind it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="your-data-stays-protected-on-the-infrastructure-you-already-sell-on"&gt;Your data stays protected on the infrastructure you already sell on&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick runs on AWS and does not use seller data to train models, not for general improvement and not for anything else. Seller data stays in the seller’s environment and never informs anyone else’s models. For a seller already operating on the Amazon marketplace, there is no new security posture to evaluate. The infrastructure is the same one protecting the seller account today, and the seller’s chosen AWS Region governs where data stays.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;The Amazon Selling Partner plugin&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Selling Partner plugin connects AWS Quick directly to live Seller Central data. After one sign-in with a Seller Central account, the plugin gives conversational access to product listings, inventory, and sales data, pulled live rather than exported and reassembled by hand. It installs like an app, with no coding required. It ships with curated Amazon skills, and it pairs with Amazon Ads MCP so campaign performance and spend sit next to the selling data. The plugin is in beta for US stores.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-145762-3" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-22255/1_permissions_setup.mp4?_=3"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1: The Selling Partner plugin consent screen, where the seller grants None, View, or Edit access per data category&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The permission screen is worth a pause. The seller grants access per data category, choosing None, View, or Edit for each, and reviews an agreement before authorizing. Nothing is all-or-nothing, and access can be narrowed or revoked later.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;2. How to redeem the Quick Plus offer&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Claim your free AWS Quick Plus subscription (12 months free plus 10 delegated users, by December 31, 2026).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Connect the Selling Partner plugin with one sign-in to your Seller Central account. It installs like an app.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ask your first question, something you would normally build a spreadsheet for.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-145762-4" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-22255/2_aws_quick_setup.mp4?_=4"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2: Connecting the Selling Partner plugin from the AWS Quick sign-up flow&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;3. What the experience looks like&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the plugin is connected, the work happens in a conversation. You ask in plain language, Quick pulls the relevant live data, and it comes back with an answer and a recommended next step. Instead of checking a dashboard, exporting a report, and reconciling it in a spreadsheet, you move from a question to an answer to an action in one place. You review what Quick proposes before anything is sent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-145762-5" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-22255/3_health_check_run.mp4?_=5"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 3: Quick diagnosing whether a sales dip comes from traffic or conversion, using live selling data&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;4. Two use cases a seller can build&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the Accelerate workshop we had sellers build real agents live, from a plain-language description, in a few minutes. Quick turns the description into a working agent, which the seller then refines in conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-case-a-inventory-launch-prep"&gt;Use case A: Inventory Launch Prep&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The seller is staging a new product into Amazon and wants the lowest total landed cost. The agent reads supplier data and weighs the fulfillment options per SKU on inbound cost, storage, and speed to sellable. It walks through placement choices and returns the output the seller picks: a shipping plan, a cost comparison, or a combined packet. It explains its reasoning and confirms before it creates anything.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-145762-6" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-22255/4_inventory_decision.mp4?_=6"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 4: The Inventory Launch Prep agent comparing FBA and AWD fulfillment per factor and recommending a hybrid approach&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-case-b-post-launch-product-health"&gt;Use case B: Post-Launch Product Health&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the launch, the agent reads four signal families through the Selling Partner plugin: listing health, inventory, sales, and traffic. It connects them to find the cause rather than just reporting numbers. Flat sales with healthy traffic points to a conversion problem. Flat sales with falling traffic points to discoverability. Good conversion with falling units points to supply. It returns a health summary per ASIN, the diagnosis with evidence, and a ranked fix list, labeling any low-confidence call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-145762-7" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-22255/5_health_verdict.mp4?_=7"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 5: The Post-Launch Product Health agent’s weekly summary, with week-over-week sales evidence&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One honest limit, handled honestly: the selling data does not include cost of goods, so the agent reports margin after Amazon fees and labels it that way. When the seller wants true gross margin, the agent asks them to bring their cost numbers into the conversation. It never presents after-fees margin as if it were gross margin.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;5. Skills and connectors in action&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Selling Partner plugin and Amazon Ads MCP bring the Amazon selling and advertising data into Quick. Other connectors in AWS Quick bring in the rest of how the business runs, organized by role. These include a second storefront (orders and products from another channel), email and calendar (draft a supplier note, track what is due), team messaging (share an update), and accounting and inventory systems (cost and stock context the selling data does not carry). Because the pieces sit together, the seller can ask one question that reaches across Amazon data and the tools around it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;6. A seller’s morning, start to finish&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At Accelerate, Richard Brown, who runs the sneaker care brand Proof Culture and sells across five stores including Amazon, showed what this looks like. He had built his own AI assistant over time, and the hard part was never the AI. It was getting his selling data connected in real time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“The hard part was never the AI. The hard part was getting my selling data into it, and that is the part that is now simple with the plugin. With Quick and the Selling Partner plugin, just connect your account and start asking questions.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Richard Brown, Proof Culture, at Amazon Accelerate 2026&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In his demo, Richard asked Quick about upcoming sneaker drops and checked whether he had enough FBA stock to cover a major release. He had Quick model how far his current inventory would carry him. Then he asked it to draft a reorder purchase order and a message to his team. A morning of research across different tools took a few minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;7. Getting started&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Claim your free year of AWS Quick Plus (by December 31, 2026).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Connect the Selling Partner plugin.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Follow the &lt;a href="https://advertising.amazon.com/API/docs/en-us/mcp/get-started-lite" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;setup instructions&lt;/a&gt; to connect the Ads MCP within AWS Quick so you can manage campaigns conversationally alongside your selling data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Connect the rest of your business by role: your other storefront, email and calendar, accounting and inventory systems, team messaging.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Start with one real question you would normally handle in a spreadsheet.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Next steps:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://quick.aws.com/sn?offer_type=sp-quick-promo" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claim your free year of AWS Quick Plus&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Learn more about AWS Quick&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
